---
name: maestro-init
disable-model-invocation: true
description: Initialize project with auto state detection
argument-hint: "[-y] [--from <source>] [--from-brainstorm SESSION-ID]"
allowed-tools:
  - read_file
  - write_file
  - shell
  - find_files
  - search
  - delegate_subagent
  - ask_user
session-mode: bootstrap
---
<!-- Open-standard mirror generated by scripts/build-agents-standard.mjs — do not edit; re-run after editing .claude/ source. -->

<purpose>
Initialize project: detect state, create `.workflow/` with project.md, state.json, config.json.
Entry point; downstream: step `roadmap` or step `brainstorm`.
</purpose>

<deferred_reading>
- [project.md](~/.maestro/templates/project.md) — read when generating project description
- [state.json](~/.maestro/templates/state.json) — read when creating initial state
- [config.json](~/.maestro/templates/config.json) — read when creating workflow configuration
</deferred_reading>

<context>
$ARGUMENTS — none for interactive mode, or `-y` with `@file` reference for auto mode.

**Flags:**

| Flag | Effect | Default |
|------|--------|---------|
| `-y` / `--yes` | Automatic mode. After config questions, runs research without further interaction. Expects idea document via @ reference. | `false` |
| `--from <source>` | Load upstream context package (brainstorm:ID, @file, or path). Consumes context-package.json to pre-fill project vision, goals, constraints, and terminology. Skips interactive questioning. Alias: `--from-brainstorm` | — |

**Load project state if exists:**
Check for `.workflow/state.json` -- loads context if project already initialized.

**Output boundary**: ALL file writes MUST target `.workflow/` (project.md, state.json, config.json, specs/) only. NEVER modify source code or files outside `.workflow/`.
</context>

<invariants>
1. **Idempotent init** — re-running init on an already-initialized project MUST detect existing `.workflow/` and warn (E002); NEVER silently overwrite existing state
2. **Scope guard** — init MUST only make initialization decisions; NEVER prejudge roadmap structure, plan scope, or implementation details
3. **All artifacts required** — init MUST NOT report completion until project.md, state.json, and config.json all exist; missing artifacts MUST be created before exit
4. **Template-driven** — deferred templates (project.md, state.json, config.json) MUST be read from `~/.maestro/templates/` and customized; NEVER generate from scratch without template
5. **Interview writes back** — all interactive decisions MUST be written to project.md/config.json before proceeding to research or completion; NEVER leave decisions unrecorded
</invariants>

<interview_protocol>
Follows @~/.maestro/workflows/interview-mechanics.md standard.

**Interaction mode**: convergent menu-driven
**Decision tree** (strict order): project type (greenfield / existing codebase onboarding) → tech stack detection and confirmation → directory structure preferences → initial configuration (specs categories, wiki bootstrap)
**Scope guard**: only init decisions; do not prejudge roadmap structure or plan scope
**Writeback target**: project.md (project description) + config.json (settings) + state.json (initial state)
**Additional skip conditions**: --from source (upstream context pre-fills decisions)
**Exit condition**: all configuration questions settled → proceed to workflow execution
</interview_protocol>

<execution>

### Phase Gates (MANDATORY, BLOCKING)

**GATE 1: Pre-flight → Interview**
- REQUIRED: `.workflow/` existence check completed.
- REQUIRED: `--from` source validated (if provided).
- BLOCKED if: E002 (greenfield conflict with existing `.workflow/`) unresolved.

**GATE 2: Interview → Research**
- REQUIRED: All interview decisions recorded in project.md and config.json.
- REQUIRED: `.workflow/` directory created with initial structure.
- BLOCKED if: interview decisions not yet written to files.

**GATE 3: Research → Completion**
- REQUIRED: All 3 required artifacts exist (project.md, state.json, config.json).
- REQUIRED: `.workflow/specs/` initialized.
- BLOCKED if: any artifact missing — write it before reporting completion.

### Pre-flight

1. Check if `.workflow/` already exists — if so, load state and warn (E002 for greenfield conflicts)
2. Validate `--from` source is accessible if provided

Follow '~/.maestro/workflows/init.md' completely.

### Artifact Verification (before completion)

---
name: analyze
prepare: analyze
commands: [maestro-analyze]
session-mode: inherited
---

# Workflow: Analyze

Multi-dimensional iterative analysis: CLI exploration → multi-perspective synthesis → discussion timeline → intent tracking → six-dimension scoring → decision extraction. Preserves macro/micro, quick, gaps, and up-to-5-round convergence semantics.

## Pipeline / FSM

```
Full mode (-q absent):
  Step 1: Setup & Scoping        → session init, dimension selection, report discussion area init
     │
  Step 2: CLI Exploration        → outputs/exploration-codebase.json, outputs/perspectives.json
     │                              (cli-explore-agent three layers + multi-CLI parallel)
     │
  Step 3: Interactive Discussion → report.md discussion section (up to 5 rounds)
     │                              (Decision Recording, Intent Coverage)
     │
  Step 4: Six-Dimension Scoring  → 6 dims × 1-5 points + risk matrix
     │
  Step 5: Synthesis & Conclusion → outputs/findings.json, report.md conclusion area
     │
  Step 6: Decision Extraction    → findings.json#decisions (Locked/Free/Deferred)

Quick mode (-q):
  Step 1: Setup (minimal)        → load upstream, skip scoping
     │
  Step 6: Decision Extraction    → findings.json#decisions
```

## Mode Determination

```
First positional arg is pure digits (^\d+$)   → micro, milestone-level deep-dive
First positional arg is non-numeric text      → macro, topic impact scope
No positional arg + has roadmap               → current milestone micro
No positional arg + no roadmap                → macro fallback
Mixed input (e.g. "1 milestone")              → handled as text, goes macro
--gaps [ISS-ID]                               → gaps, goes issue root-cause analysis (ref/issue-gaps-analyze.md)
-q                                            → quick, skip Step 2-5, jump directly to Step 6
```

macro mode additionally evaluates `scope_verdict` during Step 5 synthesis:
- `large`: 3+ independent subsystems, or a hard serial dependency barrier exists → downstream goes roadmap
- `medium`: 1-2 subsystems, parallelizable → downstream goes plan
- `small`: single file or few-file change → downstream goes plan

## Step Gates (Full mode, active when both `-q` and `--gaps` are absent)

Mandatory and blocking. Cannot advance if any gate is unmet.

**GATE: exploration-done (Step 2 → 3)**
- cli-explore-agent complete, `outputs/exploration-codebase.json` written and contains ≥1 code anchor
- ≥1 CLI delegate complete, output landed in `outputs/perspectives.json`
- discussion area records baseline confidence score

**GATE: discussion-round (Step 3 → 4)**
- discussion area contains ≥1 round of interaction with user feedback
- confidence re-scored ≥1 time and delta shown
- pressure pass completed ≥1 time

**GATE: scoring-complete (Step 4 → 5)**
- all six dimensions scored
- each dimension's score cites exploration/perspectives evidence, **not manual Read**

**GATE: intent-covered (Step 5 → 6)**
- findings.json decisions and recommendations written
- Intent Coverage Matrix has no unhandled ❌ (or user confirms defer)

---

## Process

### Step 1: Setup & Scoping

Parse mode/flags (see above). Load upstream and project context:

1. Upstream typed artifacts: read only from injected alias→path (`current-guidance`/`current-blueprint`/`latest-debug`), do not guess latest by mtime.
   - locked constraints → skip (already decided)
   - open constraints → analyze first
   - open questions → discussion seeds
2. Project specs: `maestro spec load --category arch`
3. domain glossary: read `.workflow/domain/glossary.yaml` (if it exists), use canonical terms throughout the analysis; record new term candidates in the report.
4. Existing same-milestone analyze artifacts: read their decisions, skip already-decided areas.

**quick routing**: after loading context, jump directly to Step 6.

**Scoping & dimension selection** (skipped when `-c`/`-y`, interactive AskUserQuestion, up to 3 questions):

1. **Analysis direction** (multiSelect), take 3-4 items from the dimension-direction mapping:

   | Dimension | Direction |
   |------|------|
   | architecture | System Design, Component Interactions, Technology Choices, Design Patterns |
   | implementation | Code Structure, Patterns, Error Handling, Algorithm Analysis |
   | performance | Bottlenecks, Optimization, Resource Utilization, Concurrency |
   | security | Vulnerabilities, Auth, Data Protection, Input Validation |
   | concept | Foundation, Core Mechanisms, Patterns, Trade-offs |
   | comparison | Solution Comparison, Pros/Cons, Technology Evaluation |
   | decision | Criteria, Trade-off Analysis, Risk Assessment, Impact |
   | external_research | Standard Stack, Architecture Patterns, Don't Hand-Roll, Common Pitfalls (external web search) |

   When the goal contains unfamiliar technology keywords, or the codebase has no pattern for that domain (Step 2 exploration relevant_files is empty), automatically suggest `external_research`.

2. **Analysis perspective** (multiSelect, up to 4):

   | Perspective | CLI | Focus |
   |------|-----|------|
   | Technical | gemini | implementation, code patterns, feasibility |
   | Architectural | claude | system design, scalability, interactions |
   | Business | codex | value, ROI, stakeholder impact |
   | Domain Expert | gemini | domain patterns, best practices, standards |

3. **Analysis depth** (single): Quick Overview / Standard / Deep Dive
I read all 11 files in maestro-flow documentation/. Note up front: that folder does not include guides for the actual requirement-gathering steps (brainstorm, grill, blueprint, init) — the README links to guide/quick-start-guide.en.md etc. which aren't there. So I grounded those parts in the installed workflow sources under node_modules/maestro-flow/workflows/, which is what actually executes.

1. First, how a non-Claude agent gets at any of this
Per install-guide.md, maestro install targets 60+ platforms, but the payload is not equal:

Platform	ID	What gets installed
Claude Code	claude	slash commands + skills + agents + hooks + MCP
Gemini CLI	agy	skills + agents + hooks → .gemini/
Antigravity	antigravity / antigravity-cli	skills + agents only → .antigravity/
DeepSeek	—	not a supported platform ID
Open standard	agents-standard	skills + agents → .agents/ (cross-platform spec)
Consequences you have to design around:

Only Claude gets slash commands. So /maestro, /maestro-init etc. don't exist for Antigravity/Gemini. What they get are skills (.antigravity/skills/maestro-init/SKILL.md) plus the maestro shell CLI. Everything below is reachable as plain shell commands.
DeepSeek has no install target and no cli-tools.json entry (documented tools are gemini, agy, codex, claude, opencode — see delegate-async-guide.md). A DeepSeek agent works via the agents-standard .agents/ mirror + the maestro CLI, and it cannot be a --to delegate target.
The .agents/ mirror is the portable contract. It rewrites Claude tool names into generic ones — see the frontmatter of .agents/skills/maestro-init/SKILL.md: read_file, write_file, shell, find_files, search, delegate_subagent, ask_user. ask_user is the portable stand-in for Claude's AskUserQuestion — that's the hook your agent implements to "query the user for decisions."
No hooks for Antigravity/DeepSeek means no automatic spec injection (spec-injector, keyword-spec-injector from knowledge-management-guide.md). Those agents must call maestro spec load / maestro search explicitly. Maestro anticipates this: the "Knowledge Gate" in workflows/agy-instructions.md mandates maestro search "<1-3 keywords>" --json as the first project-related tool call.
2. The four requirement input channels Maestro gives you
Everything converges into one machine-readable handoff file, context-package.json (schema context-package/1.0). That file is the real answer to your question — it's how requirement gathering survives a hop between backends.

Channel A — the user's instructions (elicitation)
Four escalating steps, all runnable as chain steps:

init (workflows/init.md) — Path A greenfield opens with "What do you want to build?" then follows the thread: challenge vague terms, surface assumptions, probe for core value / users / constraints / tech preferences / success criteria. Gate: once ≥3 dimensions gathered, asks "Ready to create project.md?"
brainstorm (workflows/brainstorm.md) — 7 sub-phases: topic analysis → role selection → 3-4 deep questions per role → conflict resolution (questions that cite specific earlier choices) → final clarification → feature decomposition into F-001-style IDs → guidance-specification.md written in RFC 2119 language (MUST/SHOULD/MAY), with §10 feature table and §11 decision tracking.
grill (workflows/grill.md) — Socratic stress-test. Notably Step 3.2 greps each proposed term against the codebase and challenges collisions: "You said 'account' — but the codebase uses UserAccount for auth. Which do you mean?"
blueprint (workflows/blueprint.md) — Phase 1.5 scores seed completeness 1-10, identifies missing dimensions (functional scope, user scenarios, NFRs, integrations, data model, error handling), then runs a max-5-round clarification loop. Output: REQ-NNN / NFR-* with a MoSCoW table, ADR-NNN, EPIC-NNN.
Channel B — the project's old documentation
--from @file → creates an import session, delegates extraction, emits context-package.json (blueprint Step 2.1, spec-generate). This is the "we have a Word/MD spec lying around" path.
--from brainstorm:ID | grill:ID | blueprint:ID → typed artifact chaining, resolved from the sealed run registry rather than by directory glob.
maestro search / maestro load over everything WikiIndexer indexes (search-system-guide.md): project.md, roadmap.md, specs/, knowhow/, issues/, domain/, sealed sessions/runs, plus Claude Code and Codex session transcripts auto-scanned from ~/.claude/projects/ and ~/.codex/sessions/.
Third-party docs get summarized into REF-*.md knowhow entries; sibling projects via maestro workspace link <path> --share spec,knowhow,domain,codebase.
Freshness is modeled, not assumed: BM25F + Ebbinghaus time decay (spec half-life 60d, knowhow 30d, issue 14d, floor 0.3), so stale old docs rank lower rather than misleading you.
Channel C — the codebase
maestro kg index — tree-sitter AST knowledge graph; queryable via maestro search "<Symbol>" --code or --kg.
maestro run skill specs-setup — scans an existing codebase and populates specs with detected conventions (init Step 2 recommends this for brownfield).
maestro domain discover — mines candidate glossary terms from interfaces/types/enums/classes/routes/docs, presents top 5 for confirmation.
init Path B explicitly infers Validated requirements from existing code ("what does the codebase already do?") and separates them from new Active requirements.
maestro explore "FIND: … SCOPE: …" — read-only fan-out search, the shell equivalent of a subagent sweep. grill Step 2.4 and analyze Step 2 both require code grounding, and flag output [LOW CONFIDENCE] if it's missing.
Channel D — querying the user for decisions
This is the most transferable piece and it's only 13 lines: workflows/interview-mechanics.md. The rules:

One decision per turn, 2-4 options, first marked (Recommended), plus an Other escape so the user can redirect.
Search-first, always: resolve from state.json → session artifacts → maestro spec/wiki → Glob/Grep/Read → Explore/delegate. "Never ask what code can answer."
Immediate writeback — each decision hits disk before the next question. No batching. (init GATE 2 blocks progress if interview decisions aren't written yet.)
A decision table with provenance: | # | Decision | Choice | Source (user/code/default) | Confidence |. source=default is always flagged LOW CONFIDENCE; source=code on a broad intent is flagged LOW CONFIDENCE and needs user confirmation.
-y does not skip the interview — it answers each question search-first, skipping only questions the code answers with high confidence. Two hard exceptions: boundary clarification for broad intent is never skipped, even with -y, and phase ambiguity is never skipped. -c skips already-answered questions.
That's a spec you can implement on any harness that can ask the user one question and write a file.

3. Concrete sequence for a Gemini / Antigravity / DeepSeek agent
Pure-shell version, no slash commands, no hooks required:


# 0. once
maestro install                      # select antigravity / agy / agents-standard
maestro kg index                     # AST graph of existing code
maestro domain init && maestro domain discover --auto

# 1. Knowledge Gate — before touching any project file
maestro search "<2-3 keywords from the ask>" --json
maestro spec load --category arch
maestro search "<keywords>" --type knowhow      # old docs, decisions, recipes

# 2. Elicit (agent drives the interview per interview-mechanics.md, writing back each answer)
maestro run skill brainstorm  ...    # or grill for stress-testing an existing proposal
                                     # → guidance-specification.md + context-package.json

# 3. Formalize
#    blueprint --from brainstorm:<ID>   → REQ/NFR/ADR/EPIC with MoSCoW
#    or roadmap --from brainstorm:<ID>  → lightweight roadmap

# 4. Scope check before planning
#    analyze "<requirement text>"  → scope_verdict: large → roadmap, medium/small → plan
Two extra levers worth knowing:

Your agent can be the worker instead of the driver. maestro delegate "PURPOSE:… TASK:… MODE: analysis" --to gemini --role brainstorm --rule planning-breakdown-task-steps --async — read-only analysis mode is the safe default for requirement work. maestro delegate message <id> "extra context" injects mid-run.
collab "<topic>" --tools gemini,claude --mode analysis fans the same requirement out to multiple CLIs and cross-verifies for consensus/conflicts — useful when you don't trust a single model's reading of an ambiguous ask.
4. Honest gaps for these three backends
No hooks on Antigravity/DeepSeek → no automatic knowledge injection; the Knowledge Gate discipline is manual and easy to skip. This is the single biggest quality delta vs. running on Claude Code.
brainstorm Step 4 (role-design-author per role), Step 4.5 (cross-role-reviewer), and init Step 3 (4 parallel workflow-project-researcher) are marked MANDATORY, NOT SUBSTITUTABLE by manual Read/Grep and assume a harness that can spawn subagents. Portable fallbacks: delegate_subagent in the .agents mirror, or maestro delegate / maestro explore from the shell.
AskUserQuestion is Claude-specific; you implement ask_user (2-4 options + Other) yourself.
interview-mechanics.md lines 11-13 are partly untranslated Chinese — a non-Claude agent reading that file gets mixed-language instructions for exactly the -y/skip and confidence rules. Worth translating locally if you standardize on it.


Worked for 23s

<session-id> means the ID of the current top-level Maestro workflow session, for example:

maestro-20260503-143022

It is not the Gemini delegate execution ID, such as:

gem-143022-a7f2

The difference is:

Identifier	Represents	Example
Maestro session ID	The complete coordinated workflow	maestro-20260503-143022
Delegate execution ID	One Gemini agent process	gem-143022-a7f2

You can view the current Maestro session with:

maestro session status

However, for user_choices.md, the session ID is optional metadata. It is only useful for tracing which workflow produced a choice:

- Source: Clarification answer Q2
- Maestro session: maestro-20260503-143022
- Gemini execution: gem-143022-a7f2

If you are running the Gemini requirements agent directly without a top-level Maestro session, omit the session field or record only the delegate execution ID:

- Source: Clarification answer Q2
- Execution: gem-143022-a7f2

So the simplest entry can just be:

## UC-001 — Supported languages

- Status: Active
- Decision: Support Urdu, Roman Urdu, and English.
- Source: Initial user query

You do not need a session ID to create or update user_choices.md. It is only for traceability.

plan.json, TASK-*.json, review.json

How are these files created?
Worked for 41s

These files are created automatically as outputs of Maestro’s plan and review workflow steps. You do not normally create them manually.

1. How plan.json and TASK-*.json are created

When Maestro runs its planning step, the planner examines:

The user’s requirements
Existing specifications
The preceding analysis.md and context.md
Relevant source code
Existing project constraints

It then generates:

plan.json
TASK-001.json
TASK-002.json
TASK-003.json
...

The flow is:

Requirements
     ↓
analyze step
     ↓
context.md + analysis.md
     ↓
plan step
     ↓
plan.json + TASK-*.json
Starting it through Maestro

Normally, you invoke the coordinator:

/maestro "Plan the implementation of the authentication requirements"

Maestro recognizes this as planning intent and dispatches a plan step.

For a complete workflow, you could provide a broader intent:

/maestro "Implement the approved authentication requirements"

Depending on project state and confirmation, Maestro can construct a chain such as:

analyze → plan → execute → verify → review

For a particular roadmap phase:

/maestro "Plan phase 1"

This routes to the equivalent internal step:

plan 1

The documents describe plan 1 as a step interface inside the Maestro session—not necessarily a standalone operating-system command.

Using an existing analysis directory

In standalone/scratch mode, Maestro can pass an analysis artifact directory into planning:

analyze "Implement JWT authentication"
plan --dir scratch/20260420-analyze-auth

The planning step reads the files in that analysis directory and creates a new plan artifact directory, conceptually similar to:

.workflow/scratch/20260420-plan-auth/
├── plan.json
├── TASK-001.json
├── TASK-002.json
└── TASK-003.json

The precise generated folder name can vary.

2. What the planning files represent

plan.json is the overall plan. It contains the plan-level information needed to coordinate execution.

Each TASK-*.json represents an individual unit of work derived from that plan:

plan.json
├── TASK-001.json
├── TASK-002.json
└── TASK-003.json

For example:

plan.json
Overall objective:
Add authentication without invalidating existing sessions.

TASK-001.json
Update the authentication interface.

TASK-002.json
Implement session-compatible authentication behavior.

TASK-003.json
Add tests for old and new sessions.

The exact JSON schema is controlled by Maestro’s planning skill. You should let Maestro generate it instead of having your requirements agent invent its own schema.

Your requirements agent should only provide the approved requirements as input. The planner is responsible for turning them into plan.json and task files.

3. How review.json is created

review.json is created by Maestro’s review step after implementation and verification.

plan.json + TASK-*.json
        ↓
execute step
        ↓
code changes + summaries
        ↓
verification.json
        ↓
review step
        ↓
review.json

The coordinator can dispatch it through a request such as:

/maestro "Review phase 1"

Internally, the step interface is:

review 1

It can also receive review options:

review 1 --level standard

Or specific dimensions:

review 1 --level standard \
  --dimensions security,architecture,correctness

According to the quality-pipeline guide, the generated artifact is stored at:

scratch/{YYYYMMDD}-review-P{N}-{slug}/review.json

Within an initialized project, this scratch location is generally managed as a Maestro workflow artifact location.

For example:

.workflow/scratch/20260807-review-P1-authentication/
└── review.json

The review result has one of three main verdicts:

Verdict	Meaning	Next action
PASS	Review passed	Continue to testing
WARN	Non-blocking concerns exist	Continue, while retaining findings
BLOCK	Critical problems exist	Create a corrective gap plan
4. What happens when review blocks the implementation

Maestro does not normally append review comments to the original plan.json.

Instead:

Original plan
     ↓
Implementation
     ↓
review.json = BLOCK
     ↓
plan --gaps
     ↓
New corrective plan + new TASK files

The documented step is:

plan 1 --gaps

That produces another plan for the unresolved review gaps. This preserves the difference between:

The original approved plan
Review findings
Corrective work created from those findings

That is cleaner than appending every reviewer comment to one plan.md.

5. Who physically writes these files?

Maestro’s coordinator manages the process:

Creates or continues a Maestro session.
Creates a Run for the selected step.
Dispatches the planning or reviewing skill.
Supplies relevant upstream artifacts.
The executing agent produces structured output.
Maestro stores that output in the artifact directory.
The next workflow step receives the artifact directory.

Therefore:

File	Produced by	When
plan.json	Maestro plan step	After analysis/requirements
TASK-*.json	Maestro plan step	Alongside the overall plan
verification.json	Built-in execution verification gate	After execution
review.json	Maestro review step	After implementation and verification

These are not generated by:

maestro kg index
maestro search
Your requirements-gathering agent
The store_knowhow tool
The Gemini delegate automatically

A Gemini-based planner or reviewer may perform the reasoning if integrated into that step, but it must run under the relevant Maestro workflow contract so Maestro receives the expected artifacts.

How this fits your system

Your agents should have separate responsibilities:

Requirements agent
    → requirements specification
    → confirmed user choices

Planner agent / Maestro plan step
    → plan.json
    → TASK-*.json

Implementer
    → code changes

Verification and reviewer
    → verification.json
    → review.json

Gap planner, when blocked
    → corrective plan.json
    → corrective TASK-*.json

So, you do not need a separate agent to manually create these three file types if you are using Maestro’s native planning and quality pipelines. You only need to ensure that the finalized requirements and user choices are provided as upstream context to Maestro’s plan step.

This behavior is documented in the 
command usage guide, 
coordinator guide, and 
quality pipeline guide.

Worked for 1m 49s

Yes. Maestro-Flow can run a single plan step, generate plan.json plus TASK-*.json, and stop before implementation. This is the cleanest way to build your planner.

The important distinction is:

Planner agent = Codex CLI or Claude Code doing the reasoning
Maestro plan step = workflow contract that structures and stores the result
Recommended flow

Maestro documents plan as a single-step chain. Its canonical outputs are:

plan.json
TASK-001.json
TASK-002.json
...

You should preserve both types of files because later Maestro execution steps consume the individual task artifacts, not merely the overall plan.

Running only Maestro’s plan step

From a Maestro-enabled coding client, give /maestro explicit planning-only intent:

/maestro "Create an implementation plan only. Use the approved requirements
from .workflow/handoffs/REQ-001/. Generate the planning artifacts and stop.
Do not implement, review, test, or modify production code."

Maestro’s intent router recognizes plan and creates a single-step chain:

{
  "chain_name": "plan",
  "steps": [
    {
      "type": "skill",
      "skill": "plan",
      "args": "--dir .workflow/handoffs/REQ-001"
    }
  ]
}

Conceptually, this runs:

plan --dir .workflow/handoffs/REQ-001

However, plan --dir is a Maestro workflow step interface, not necessarily a standalone shell command. The documentation says first-tier steps should be dispatched through /maestro or /maestro-next. See the 
command-usage guide and 
coordinator guide.

What --dir means

--dir tells the plan step where to find its upstream input:

plan --dir <upstream-artifact-directory>

Maestro normally uses an analysis artifact directory containing files such as:

context.md
analysis.md

Your requirements agent is external to that standard pipeline, so you need a small handoff contract between it and the Maestro plan step.

The supplied documentation does not guarantee that Maestro can consume any arbitrary requirements JSON or Markdown structure. Therefore, do not connect the planner directly to an unfinalized output format yet.

Use a requirements handoff directory

For now, define the boundary as a directory rather than committing to one exact requirements format:

.workflow/handoffs/REQ-001/
├── handoff.json
├── requirements.md
└── user_choices.md

A minimal handoff.json could be:

{
  "schema_version": "requirements-handoff/1.0",
  "request_id": "REQ-001",
  "status": "complete",
  "requirements_file": "requirements.md",
  "user_choices_file": "user_choices.md",
  "open_questions": [],
  "ready_for_planning": true
}

The requirements.md representation can change later, but it should eventually communicate these concepts:

Problem and goal
Requested scope
Functional requirements
Non-functional requirements
Constraints
Confirmed user decisions
Assumptions
Out-of-scope items
Acceptance criteria
Relevant current code areas
Conflicts and resolutions
Remaining open questions

Those concepts already match your current requirements-agent output.

Planning readiness gate

Before launching the planner, your supervisor should check:

requirements status == COMPLETE
ready_for_planning == true
open_questions == []
acceptance criteria exist
no unresolved material conflict exists

If the requirements agent returns:

STATUS: INPUT_REQUIRED

the planner must not run.

This keeps planning from being based on incomplete or provisional requirements.

Codex CLI versus Claude Code

Maestro’s delegate system supports both:

--to codex
--to claude

It also supports:

--role plan
--mode write
--cd <project-root>

For example, a custom Codex planner can be launched with:

maestro delegate \
  "Create an implementation plan from the approved requirements in
  .workflow/handoffs/REQ-001/. Do not implement anything. Produce the required
  planning artifacts in the designated output directory." \
  --to codex \
  --role plan \
  --mode write \
  --cd . \
  --async

The Claude equivalent is:

maestro delegate \
  "Create an implementation plan from the approved requirements in
  .workflow/handoffs/REQ-001/. Do not implement anything. Produce the required
  planning artifacts in the designated output directory." \
  --to claude \
  --role plan \
  --mode write \
  --cd . \
  --async

Both are supported according to the 
delegate guide.

Important limitation of maestro delegate

This command:

maestro delegate ... --role plan

does not necessarily execute Maestro’s canonical plan workflow step.

It guarantees only that:

The selected CLI worker is launched.
It receives a planning role.
It can operate in the chosen directory.
It can write files when --mode write is used.

Unless the prompt or surrounding wrapper enforces Maestro’s planning contract, the agent might return a Markdown plan or ordinary prose instead of valid:

plan.json
TASK-*.json

Therefore, there are two possible designs:

Design	Select Codex/Claude explicitly?	Canonical Maestro artifacts?
Native /maestro single-step plan	Not clearly documented per invocation	Yes
maestro delegate --to codex/claude --role plan	Yes	Only if you enforce and validate the contract

The Maestro documentation exposes:

/maestro ... --exec auto|cli|internal

but it does not clearly document a /maestro plan --executor codex option. The explicit --executor codex flag shown in the docs belongs to Odyssey’s larger planex workflow, which would exceed your planning-only scope.

Best design for your system

I recommend making the planner agent a thin wrapper around Maestro’s native plan step:

PlannerAgent
├── verify requirements handoff
├── create a planning-only Maestro Session
├── dispatch exactly one plan Run
├── allow Codex/Claude to execute that Run
├── validate plan.json
├── validate TASK-*.json
└── stop

Use Maestro’s Session/Run machinery to own:

Planning lifecycle
Artifact location
Plan and task schemas
Traceability
Resume state
Downstream compatibility

Use your own orchestrator to own:

Whether Codex or Claude is selected
Requirements readiness checks
The requirements-to-Maestro input adapter
Rejection of invalid planner output
What to finalize next

You do not need to finalize the entire requirements-agent output immediately. First finalize the semantic handoff contract:

status
goal
scope
requirements
constraints
user decisions
acceptance criteria
open questions
relevant project evidence

Then you can choose whether its physical representation is:

requirements.md
requirements.json
Both Markdown and JSON
Maestro-compatible context.md and analysis.md

My preferred arrangement is:

Requirements agent
    → requirements.json as machine-readable canonical output
    → requirements.md as human-readable output

Adapter
    → Maestro-compatible upstream artifact directory

Maestro plan step
    → plan.json
    → TASK-*.json

So the direct answer is: yes, run only Maestro’s single-step plan chain. Let Maestro define the plan artifacts, while your coordinator supplies a normalized requirements handoff and chooses whether Codex CLI or Claude Code performs the planning reasoning.

Worked for 2m 21s

Yes. Maestro Flow can run only its planning stage, without implementing the plan.

The important detail is that in the current Maestro architecture, plan is an internal workflow step dispatched through /maestro; it is not a separate /maestro-plan slash command. The coordinator recognizes a planning-only request and creates a single-step plan chain. Maestro’s current command guide documents this structure.

1. Run only Maestro’s plan stage

Inside Claude Code, run:

/maestro "Run only the plan step. Create the planning artifacts, then stop. Do not execute or modify source code."

For an initialized Maestro project and a specific milestone:

/maestro "Run only plan 1 for milestone 1. Create the planning artifacts, then stop. Do not execute."

This should produce a single-step chain equivalent to:

plan 1

Do not use this:

/maestro --dry-run "plan the project"

--dry-run only previews which Maestro chain would run. It does not actually run the planner or create plan.json.

2. Outputs created by the plan step

The planning artifact is normally stored in a generated directory under:

.workflow/scratch/<generated-plan-directory>/

The principal outputs are:

plan.json
TASK-001.json
TASK-002.json
TASK-003.json
...

Their roles are:

Output	Purpose
plan.json	The overall implementation plan and task organization
TASK-*.json	Individual executable tasks derived from the plan
PLN-{NNN} entry	The registered Maestro planning artifact
Session status	Records that the single planning step was completed

Because Maestro generates the artifact directory name, your orchestration code should obtain the output path from the command result or Maestro’s artifact registry rather than guessing it. The coordinator also records session information under a path resembling:

.workflow/.maestro/maestro-<timestamp>/status.json

The supplied 
command usage guide confirms that the plan stage produces plan.json + TASK-*.json and registers a PLN-{NNN} artifact.

3. Use requirements produced by requirements_gathering

Yes—but Maestro must be told where those requirements are stored. It will not automatically discover another independent agent’s output unless both agents are operating through the same registered Maestro session/artifact chain.

The most reliable handoff is a directory such as:

.workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/
└── context.md

Then run:

/maestro "Run only this chain step: plan --dir .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering. Use context.md as the authoritative requirements. Create plan.json and TASK-*.json, then stop. Do not execute."

This corresponds conceptually to:

plan --dir .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering

The documentation describes --dir as a way to supply an upstream artifact directory directly, including in standalone mode where initialization and a roadmap are not required.

4. If the requirements output is Markdown

Recommended structure:

.workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/
├── context.md
└── analysis.md        # optional

Command:

/maestro "Run only plan --dir .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering. Treat context.md as the approved requirements and analysis.md as supporting analysis. Stop after producing the planning artifacts."

If your agent produces requirements.md, either rename/normalize it to context.md, or reference it explicitly:

/maestro "Plan only. Read .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/requirements.md as the authoritative requirements. Produce plan.json and TASK-*.json, then stop without executing."

The first option is preferable because context.md matches Maestro’s native analyze-to-plan handoff.

5. If the output is a JSON file

Directory:

.workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/
└── requirements.json

Command:

/maestro "Run only the plan step. Read requirements from .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/requirements.json. Create plan.json and TASK-*.json, then stop. Do not execute."

However, Maestro’s documented native upstream format is a directory containing artifacts such as context.md and analysis.md. Therefore, for a stable integration, I recommend converting the JSON into context.md before invoking the planner.

For example:

requirements_gathering agent
        ↓
requirements.json or requirements.md
        ↓
handoff normalizer
        ↓
.workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/context.md
        ↓
Maestro plan --dir
        ↓
plan.json + TASK-*.json
6. Suggested temporary requirements contract

Since your requirements agent’s output is not finalized, use a small normalized schema that can be represented as either Markdown or JSON:

{
  "objective": "",
  "requirements": [],
  "constraints": [],
  "acceptance_criteria": [],
  "user_choices": [],
  "open_questions": [],
  "out_of_scope": [],
  "source_files": []
}

The planner should follow this rule:

requirements, constraints, acceptance_criteria, and user_choices are authoritative.
open_questions must not be silently assumed.
out_of_scope must not appear in implementation tasks.
Each task should map back to one or more requirements or acceptance criteria.
7. Claude Code versus Codex CLI

For the first implementation, I recommend running the Maestro coordinator through Claude Code because Maestro’s documented core installation still lists Claude Code as required, while Codex is an optional multi-agent backend. The install documentation says Maestro can install agents, skills, hooks, and MCP support for Codex, but the /maestro orchestration entry is documented primarily as a Claude Code slash command. Official repository

A non-interactive Claude Code planner invocation could be:

claude -p '/maestro Run only the plan step. Read requirements from .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering/context.md. Produce the planning artifacts and stop without executing.'

For Codex, first verify that the plan skill was installed:

maestro skills --platform codex

If plan appears, the Codex-side invocation would follow the installed-skill interface, for example:

codex exec '$plan --dir .workflow/handoffs/requirements_gathering'

However, the supplied Maestro documentation does not provide a fully documented standalone command such as:

/maestro ... --executor codex

for a plan-only run. It documents --executor codex for Odyssey’s complete planex lifecycle, which also executes and verifies, so that is unsuitable for your planner-only agent.

Therefore, the safest initial architecture is:

requirements_gathering agent
        ↓
context.md
        ↓
Claude Code + /maestro single plan step
        ↓
plan.json + TASK-*.json

Later, you can add a Codex adapter that invokes the installed plan skill while preserving exactly the same input and output contract.

12-command-usage-guide.en.md
Document
The output plan.json + TASK-*.json files....do these have any way of stating which task should be completed first and which tasks can be done in parallel?
Worked for 1m 49s

Yes. Maestro’s plan format explicitly represents both task order and parallelism.

The relationship is expressed through three mechanisms:

Field/artifact	Meaning
depends_on or deps in TASK-*.json	Tasks that must finish before this task can start
wave in each task	The execution stage containing the task
waves in plan.json / waves.json	Groups tasks that can execute concurrently

For example:

// TASK-001.json
{
  "id": "TASK-001",
  "title": "Create authentication service",
  "depends_on": [],
  "wave": 1,
  "status": "pending"
}
// TASK-002.json
{
  "id": "TASK-002",
  "title": "Create login UI",
  "depends_on": [],
  "wave": 1,
  "status": "pending"
}
// TASK-003.json
{
  "id": "TASK-003",
  "title": "Connect login UI to authentication service",
  "depends_on": ["TASK-001", "TASK-002"],
  "wave": 2,
  "status": "pending"
}

The corresponding wave structure would be:

{
  "waves": [
    {
      "wave": 1,
      "tasks": ["TASK-001", "TASK-002"]
    },
    {
      "wave": 2,
      "tasks": ["TASK-003"]
    }
  ]
}

This means:

TASK-001 and TASK-002 have no dependencies.
Both belong to Wave 1, so they can run in parallel.
TASK-003 depends on both tasks.
It belongs to Wave 2 and cannot begin until both Wave 1 dependencies complete successfully.

Conceptually:

Maestro’s execution workflow processes waves serially but tasks inside each wave in parallel:

for each wave in order:
    run eligible tasks in that wave in parallel
    wait for completion
    proceed to the next wave

The planner is specifically instructed to:

Define dependencies between tasks.
Group tasks into waves.
Order waves by priority.
Avoid circular dependencies.
Avoid putting tasks that modify the same files in the same wave.
Use dependencies only when one task genuinely needs another task’s output.

The plan-checker validates the resulting dependency graph and blocks plans containing circular dependencies. Maestro’s current source documents this in its planning workflow and execution workflow.

Current Maestro versions may also create:

plan.json
tasks/TASK-001.json
tasks/TASK-002.json
waves.json
dependency-graph.json
collision-report.json
plan-check.json

A small version-related detail: some Maestro files use the field name:

"depends_on": ["TASK-001"]

while the newer plan-task/1.0 skeleton uses:

"deps": ["TASK-001"]

Your orchestration adapter should either use the schema version declared in _meta.schema or temporarily accept both field names:

const dependencies = task.deps ?? task.depends_on ?? [];

Therefore, the planner output is not just a list of tasks. It can act as a dependency-aware execution plan from which your supervisor determines:

What must run first.
What is currently ready.
What can run concurrently.
What becomes blocked when an upstream task fails.
When the next execution wave can begin.



6:15 PM

I m implementing an Orchestrator Agent and Merger Agent in the system. The outputs of the planner agent (plan.json, TASK-*.json) are the inputs of the Orchestrator agent. This reads the plan's and the tasks' files and runs the gemini cli-based coding subagents' waves. When one wave is completed it dispatches the next until all tasks have been completed. If there is more than one subagent in a wave, each of these coding subagents work in a separate git worktree. Each of the coding agents will have access to the knowledge graph, knowledge base markdown files and the learnings markdown file from maestro-flow, which they will use to generate the response. Also, if it finds any new findings, the coding agent should report it to the learnings file.

After each wave, a merger agent merges the git worktrees of each coding agent, and if there is any conflict the merger agent resolves it. Also, if it finds any new findings, the merger agent should report it to the learnings file.

How can maestro-flow help me in creating these 2 agents?

Worked for 52s

Maestro-Flow can provide most of the infrastructure for your Orchestrator Agent and Merger Agent, but your exact workflow still needs a custom control layer.

The best approach is:

Use Maestro-Flow for planning artifacts, Gemini CLI dispatch, execution tracking, knowledge retrieval, learning storage, and parts of worktree management.
Implement your own scheduler for reading plan.json/TASK-*.json, forming waves, creating task-level worktrees, and invoking the Merger Agent after every wave.
Implement your own AI-assisted conflict-resolution policy around Git because Maestro’s documented merge command does not promise fully autonomous conflict resolution.
1. Recommended architecture

The Orchestrator Agent should be deterministic code—TypeScript or Python—not merely an LLM prompt. The LLM-based Gemini agents should perform coding, while your program controls scheduling, state, retries, worktrees, and process execution.

2. How Maestro-Flow helps the Orchestrator Agent
A. Planning input

Maestro’s planning stage already produces:

plan.json
TASK-001.json
TASK-002.json
TASK-003.json
...

The documentation identifies these as the normal input to the execute stage. See 
command-usage-guide.en.md.

Your Orchestrator should validate that each task contains at least:

{
  "id": "TASK-002",
  "title": "Implement authentication service",
  "description": "...",
  "dependencies": ["TASK-001"],
  "acceptance_criteria": [],
  "verification_commands": [],
  "allowed_paths": [],
  "status": "pending"
}

If Maestro’s generated task schema uses different field names, add a normalization adapter:

interface NormalizedTask {
  id: string;
  description: string;
  dependencies: string[];
  acceptanceCriteria: string[];
  verificationCommands: string[];
  allowedPaths: string[];
}

Do not couple the scheduler directly to one version of Maestro’s JSON schema.

B. Wave calculation

Maestro describes Wave as its dependency-parallelism coordination pattern. However, the supplied documentation does not provide a stable, directly invokable Wave CLI command or a detailed Wave API contract.

Therefore, calculate the waves yourself from task dependencies:

Wave 1: tasks with no incomplete dependencies
Wave 2: tasks whose dependencies completed in Wave 1
Wave 3: tasks unblocked by Waves 1–2

The scheduler logic is essentially:

while (unfinishedTasksExist()) {
  const ready = pendingTasks.filter(task =>
    task.dependencies.every(id => state.tasks[id].status === "completed")
  );

  if (ready.length === 0) {
    throw new Error("Dependency cycle or failed blocking dependency");
  }

  await executeWave(ready);
  await runMergerAgent(ready);
}

You should also detect cycles before execution using topological sorting.

C. Dispatching Gemini CLI coding agents

Maestro’s delegate command is highly useful here. It supports:

Gemini dispatch
Asynchronous parallel execution
Per-agent working directory
Write mode
Custom execution IDs
Timeouts
Status/output collection
Cancellation
Follow-up messages
Session resume

Example for one task:

maestro delegate \
  "Implement TASK-001 using the attached task specification. Commit all completed changes before finishing." \
  --to gemini \
  --role implement \
  --mode write \
  --cd "/absolute/path/to/worktree-TASK-001" \
  --id "TASK-001-attempt-1" \
  --timeout 1200000 \
  --async

The Orchestrator stores the returned execution ID:

{
  "task_id": "TASK-001",
  "delegate_execution_id": "TASK-001-attempt-1",
  "status": "running",
  "worktree": "/repo/.worktrees/TASK-001",
  "branch": "agent/TASK-001",
  "attempt": 1
}

It can then use:

maestro delegate status TASK-001-attempt-1
maestro delegate output TASK-001-attempt-1 --full
maestro delegate tail TASK-001-attempt-1
maestro delegate cancel TASK-001-attempt-1

These commands are documented in 
delegate-async-guide.md.

D. Constructing the coding-agent prompt

Use Maestro’s documented six-field prompt structure:

PURPOSE:
Complete TASK-001 while preserving the requirements and architectural constraints.

TASK:
1. Read the task specification.
2. Load relevant Maestro knowledge.
3. Inspect the knowledge graph for affected components.
4. Implement only the assigned task.
5. Run the task's verification commands.
6. Commit the changes.
7. Write a structured task result.

MODE:
write

CONTEXT:
Task: @TASK-001.json
Plan: @plan.json
Knowledge: @.workflow/specs/
Knowhow: @.workflow/knowhow/
Knowledge graph: @.workflow/codebase/knowledge-graph.json

EXPECTED:
A Git commit plus task-result.json containing status, commit SHA,
changed files, tests, decisions, findings, and unresolved issues.

CONSTRAINTS:
Do not modify files outside allowed_paths.
Do not merge branches.
Do not start another task.
Do not directly edit shared orchestration state.

Pass it through:

maestro delegate "$(cat task-prompt.txt)" \
  --to gemini \
  --role implement \
  --mode write \
  --cd "$TASK_WORKTREE" \
  --id "$EXECUTION_ID" \
  --async

In your implementation, avoid shell interpolation by using a process API such as Node’s spawn() with an argument array.

E. Knowledge access

Maestro gives you three useful knowledge layers:

Layer	Use
.workflow/specs/	Architectural constraints, conventions and learnings
.workflow/knowhow/	Full recipes, decisions, tips and previous solutions
Knowledge graph	Symbols, dependencies, layers and code relationships

Maestro’s knowledge system can automatically inject coding and learning categories for recognized internal agent types. But you should not assume that every independently launched Gemini process receives all knowledge automatically.

Your Orchestrator should explicitly load or reference the relevant context:

maestro spec load --category coding
maestro spec load --category learning
maestro wiki search "authentication middleware"

Then either:

include the retrieved information in the task prompt; or
write a small task-context.md into the worktree and reference it in the prompt.

Knowledge management and injection are described in 
knowledge-management-guide.md.

3. Task-level worktrees require custom handling

Maestro provides:

/maestro-fork -m 2
/maestro-merge -m 2

However, these commands are documented as operating on a milestone-level worktree, while your design needs a separate worktree for every parallel task in a wave.

Therefore:

You can reuse Maestro’s worktree concepts.
You should not directly rely on /maestro-fork for task isolation unless you modify or extend it.
The simplest solution is to let your Orchestrator call native Git commands.

For example:

git worktree add \
  "/repo/.worktrees/TASK-001" \
  -b "agent/TASK-001" \
  "$WAVE_BASE_SHA"

Every worktree in the same wave must start from the same base commit:

{
  "wave": 2,
  "base_sha": "abc123...",
  "tasks": ["TASK-003", "TASK-004"]
}

This matters because creating later worktrees from already-modified branches would silently make the supposedly parallel tasks sequentially dependent.

4. How Maestro-Flow helps the Merger Agent
A. Existing merge support

Maestro offers:

/maestro-merge -m 2 --dry-run
/maestro-merge -m 2 --no-cleanup
/maestro-merge -m 2 --continue

It performs two broad phases:

Git merge
Maestro artifact synchronization and registry reconciliation

That is useful, but it is milestone-oriented. See 
command-usage-guide.en.md.

For your task branches, the Merger Agent should use native Git merges, then use Maestro for artifact/knowledge reconciliation where applicable.

B. Recommended merge sequence

Merge task branches one at a time in a deterministic order:

TASK-003
TASK-004
TASK-005

Do not let multiple processes merge into the integration branch simultaneously.

A safe wave merge process is:

Create an integration branch from the wave’s base commit.
Validate each task result.
Merge each task branch with --no-ff.
If a conflict occurs, invoke the Merger Agent.
Run verification after each merge.
Run the complete wave verification suite.
Advance the main orchestration branch only after the wave passes.

Example:

git switch -c integrate/wave-002 "$WAVE_BASE_SHA"
git merge --no-ff agent/TASK-003
git merge --no-ff agent/TASK-004

The merger should receive:

plan.json
relevant TASK-*.json files
each task’s result file
each task’s commit SHA
the conflict files
git diff --merge
relevant knowledge and architectural constraints
acceptance criteria and verification commands
C. Conflict-resolution prompt
PURPOSE:
Resolve the merge conflict between TASK-003 and TASK-004 without
discarding either task's required behavior.

TASK:
1. Read both task specifications and results.
2. Inspect every conflicted file.
3. Determine the semantic intent of both changes.
4. Resolve the conflict.
5. Run the affected tests.
6. Record the resolution decision and any new finding.
7. Commit the resolution.

MODE:
write

CONTEXT:
Plan: @plan.json
Tasks: @TASK-003.json, @TASK-004.json
Results: @task-results/TASK-003.json, @task-results/TASK-004.json
Constraints: @.workflow/specs/
Conflict state: current Git index

EXPECTED:
A resolved Git index, passing verification, merge commit,
and merge-result.json.

CONSTRAINTS:
Do not remove required behavior merely to make Git merge clean.
Do not modify unrelated files.
Do not claim success unless all conflict markers are removed
and verification commands pass.

A simple textual conflict may be resolved by Gemini, but the result must always be checked deterministically:

git diff --check
git grep -nE '^(<<<<<<<|=======|>>>>>>>)'
npm test
npm run lint
npm run typecheck
5. Handling learnings.md correctly

Allowing every parallel worktree to append directly to:

.workflow/specs/learnings.md

will frequently create merge conflicts. Every agent may modify the same end of the same Markdown file.

A better design is:

Task agent
  → task-result.json.findings[]
  → Merger Agent validates/deduplicates findings
  → one centralized write to learnings.md after the wave

Example result:

{
  "task_id": "TASK-003",
  "status": "completed",
  "commit_sha": "abc123",
  "verification": {
    "passed": true,
    "commands": []
  },
  "findings": [
    {
      "type": "gotcha",
      "title": "Token refresh requires the email field",
      "description": "...",
      "keywords": ["auth", "refresh-token"],
      "evidence": ["src/auth/refresh.ts:42"]
    }
  ]
}

After merging, the Merger Agent consolidates only genuinely reusable findings into Maestro knowledge.

Maestro supports:

structured entries in specs/learnings.md;
knowhow documents;
deduplication of findings;
staged knowledge candidates;
review and promotion;
automatic knowledge harvesting.

For robust operation, prefer the reviewable lifecycle:

task finding
    → staged candidate
    → merger validates
    → knowledge review
    → promote

Rather than uncontrolled Markdown appends from every parallel agent.

For simple atomic findings, Maestro also provides the store_knowhow tool and spec/knowhow commands. The learning system is explained in 
learn-tools-guide.md.

6. State files you should add

Maestro’s canonical Session/Run system can preserve lifecycle information, but your wave scheduler needs its own explicit state.

I recommend:

.workflow/orchestration/
├── execution-state.json
├── waves/
│   ├── WAVE-001.json
│   └── WAVE-002.json
├── task-results/
│   ├── TASK-001.json
│   └── TASK-002.json
└── merge-results/
    ├── WAVE-001.json
    └── WAVE-002.json

Example wave state:

{
  "id": "WAVE-002",
  "base_sha": "abc123",
  "status": "merging",
  "tasks": {
    "TASK-003": {
      "status": "completed",
      "branch": "agent/TASK-003",
      "worktree": "/repo/.worktrees/TASK-003",
      "execution_id": "TASK-003-attempt-1",
      "commit_sha": "def456"
    }
  },
  "merge": {
    "status": "running",
    "integration_branch": "integrate/WAVE-002",
    "merged_tasks": [],
    "conflicted_tasks": []
  }
}

Write state atomically—temporary file followed by rename—so a crash cannot leave malformed JSON.

7. Exact division of responsibilities
Responsibility	Maestro-Flow	Your custom code
Generate plan.json and tasks	Yes	Validation adapter
Recognize dependency parallelism	Conceptually yes	Reliable DAG/wave scheduler
Launch Gemini CLI agents	Yes, through maestro delegate	Build prompts and track IDs
Async lifecycle/status/cancel	Yes	Apply retry policy
Resume delegate sessions	Yes	Persist session/execution IDs
Load specs and knowledge	Yes	Explicitly assemble task context
Code knowledge graph	Yes	Query it for each task
Milestone worktrees	Yes	—
Task-level worktrees	Not documented	Yes
Milestone merge	Yes	—
Per-wave task-branch merging	Not directly	Yes
Autonomous conflict resolution	Not guaranteed	Merger Agent
Deterministic verification	Maestro supports pipelines	Enforce at each wave
Learning capture	Yes	Centralize and deduplicate
Crash-safe wave recovery	Session facilities help	Custom execution state
Bottom line

Maestro-Flow can serve as the execution and knowledge infrastructure underneath both agents:

plan.json and TASK-*.json provide the work definitions.
maestro delegate --to gemini --async --cd <worktree> launches each coding subagent.
Maestro specs, knowhow, search, and KG provide the agents’ context.
Maestro’s knowledge system stores reusable findings.
/maestro-merge provides a useful model for merge and artifact reconciliation.

But your system still needs a custom controller for:

dependency parsing and wave calculation;
task-level Git worktree creation;
parallel process tracking;
per-wave integration branches;
deterministic branch merge order;
AI-assisted conflict resolution;
test gates;
retries and crash recovery;
centralized learning consolidation.

That custom layer is effectively your Orchestrator Agent and Merger Agent; Maestro-Flow supplies their lower-level tools rather than completely replacing either one.

1 more
The output of the Merger Agent should be a Reviewer Agent, which reviews it against the original context and the tasks files. This agent is claude code or codex cli based. If needed, adds new implementation comments that are given back to the same coding subagent (same session id), its worktree's work is reverted/deleted, and the process cycles. Otherwise, the orchestrator continues with the next wave. 
When all waves are completed, the Supervisor Agent reviews the final code with the original context.md and checks if all the requirements have been fullfilled. This agent is claude code or codex cli based. If not satisfactory, it provides comments to planner agent and the process cycles.
How can maestro-flow help me in these?
Worked for 1m 0s

Maestro-Flow can support both the Reviewer and Supervisor Agents through CLI delegation, session resumption, quality artifacts, knowledge injection, and review/fix loops. However, your Orchestrator still needs to control branch rollback, attribution of review findings, and restarting the correct coding agents.

The resulting lifecycle should be:

1. How Maestro helps the Reviewer Agent
Launching Claude Code or Codex CLI

After the Merger produces a temporary integration branch/worktree, your Orchestrator can launch the Reviewer through maestro delegate:

maestro delegate \
  "Review the merged implementation for WAVE-002 using context.md, plan.json, the assigned TASK files, task results, and the Git diff. Write wave-review.json." \
  --to codex \
  --role review \
  --mode analysis \
  --cd "/repo/.worktrees/review-WAVE-002" \
  --rule analysis-review-code-quality \
  --id "review-WAVE-002-attempt-1" \
  --timeout 1200000 \
  --async

For Claude Code:

maestro delegate \
  "Review WAVE-002 and write wave-review.json." \
  --to claude \
  --role review \
  --mode analysis \
  --cd "/repo/.worktrees/review-WAVE-002" \
  --id "review-WAVE-002-attempt-1" \
  --async

Maestro provides:

Claude Code and Codex CLI selection through --to;
a dedicated review role;
read-only analysis mode;
explicit working-directory selection;
asynchronous execution;
timeout and cancellation;
status and output retrieval;
session resumption and follow-up messages.

These capabilities are documented in the 
delegate async guide.

Reviewer context

The Reviewer should receive:

context.md
plan.json
TASK-003.json
TASK-004.json
task-results/TASK-003.json
task-results/TASK-004.json
merge-results/WAVE-002.json
verification results
Git diff from wave_base_sha to integration HEAD
relevant Maestro specs and knowhow

Its prompt should explicitly require traceability:

PURPOSE:
Determine whether the merged implementation of WAVE-002 correctly
satisfies the original context and every assigned task.

TASK:
1. Read context.md and plan.json.
2. Read every TASK file in WAVE-002.
3. Inspect the merged diff and task-result files.
4. Verify every acceptance criterion.
5. Run or inspect the required verification commands.
6. Attribute every finding to the responsible task or mark it as
   an integration-level problem.
7. Produce wave-review.json.

MODE:
analysis

EXPECTED:
A structured verdict of APPROVE, CHANGES_REQUESTED, or REPLAN_REQUIRED.

CONSTRAINTS:
Do not edit the code.
Do not judge tasks independently only; inspect their merged interaction.
Every finding must contain evidence and a responsible task when identifiable.
Recommended review output
{
  "wave_id": "WAVE-002",
  "attempt": 1,
  "base_sha": "abc123",
  "integration_sha": "def456",
  "verdict": "CHANGES_REQUESTED",
  "requirements_checked": [
    {
      "requirement_id": "REQ-014",
      "status": "failed",
      "evidence": ["src/auth/token.ts:74"],
      "task_ids": ["TASK-003"]
    }
  ],
  "findings": [
    {
      "id": "REV-W002-001",
      "severity": "blocking",
      "category": "correctness",
      "scope": "task",
      "owner_task_ids": ["TASK-003"],
      "description": "Refresh-token expiry is not validated.",
      "required_change": "Reject expired refresh tokens before rotation.",
      "evidence": ["src/auth/token.ts:74"],
      "verification": ["npm test -- auth-refresh"]
    }
  ],
  "approved_task_ids": ["TASK-004"],
  "affected_task_ids": ["TASK-003"]
}

Use three verdicts:

Verdict	Orchestrator action
APPROVE	Accept the integration branch and dispatch the next wave
CHANGES_REQUESTED	Send findings to affected coder or merger, then review again
REPLAN_REQUIRED	Return the gap to the Planner because the existing task definition is insufficient

Maestro’s native quality pipeline uses the similar PASS, WARN, and BLOCK model and produces review.json. A blocked review normally routes into plan --gaps → execute → review. See the 
quality pipeline guide.

2. Correctly returning comments to the same coding agent

Maestro supports two relevant mechanisms.

Follow-up to a completed delegate
maestro delegate message TASK-003-attempt-1 \
  "Reviewer requested changes. Read reviewer-feedback/REV-W002-001.json, implement the correction, run the listed verification commands, and commit the corrected implementation." \
  --delivery after_complete

This queues another turn for the delegate after its previous work has completed.

Explicitly resume its CLI session
maestro delegate \
  "Address the blocking review findings in reviewer-feedback/TASK-003.json. Reinspect the current files because the worktree has been reconstructed from the wave base." \
  --to gemini \
  --role implement \
  --mode write \
  --cd "/repo/.worktrees/TASK-003" \
  --resume "$CODER_SESSION_ID" \
  --id "TASK-003-attempt-2" \
  --async

Keep these identifiers separate:

{
  "task_id": "TASK-003",
  "agent": "gemini",
  "native_session_id": "gemini-session-xyz",
  "executions": [
    {
      "execution_id": "TASK-003-attempt-1",
      "attempt": 1
    },
    {
      "execution_id": "TASK-003-attempt-2",
      "attempt": 2
    }
  ]
}

The native session ID preserves the coding agent’s conversation. The execution ID identifies a particular Maestro invocation.

3. Do not merge a wave permanently before review

The Merger should not immediately advance main or the canonical orchestration branch. Instead:

wave base
   ├── agent/TASK-003
   ├── agent/TASK-004
   └── integrate/WAVE-002/attempt-1

The integration branch remains provisional until the Reviewer approves it.

On approval

Move the canonical wave baseline to the reviewed integration commit:

accepted_wave_sha = reviewed integration_sha
next_wave_base_sha = accepted_wave_sha
On task-local rejection

For an issue owned by TASK-003:

Preserve its agent session ID, review findings, result file and commit SHA.
Discard the provisional integration attempt.
Remove/reconstruct only the TASK-003 worktree.
Create agent/TASK-003/attempt-2 from the original wave base.
Resume the same Gemini session with the review feedback.
Rebuild a new integration branch using approved task commits plus the corrected commit.
Run the Reviewer again.

The resumed coder must be told that its files were reconstructed. Its conversational memory describes the previous version, while its filesystem now contains the clean wave baseline.

On integration-level rejection

Some problems cannot be attributed to a single coding agent—for example, two individually valid changes expose incompatible APIs.

In this case, do not arbitrarily send the problem to one coder. Either:

resume all affected coding agents with coordinated feedback; or
invoke the Merger as an integration-fixer on the provisional integration worktree.

Example:

maestro delegate \
  "Resolve the integration-level findings in reviewer-feedback/WAVE-002.json. Preserve the requirements of all affected tasks, run the listed tests, and commit the fix." \
  --to codex \
  --role implement \
  --mode write \
  --cd "/repo/.worktrees/integrate-WAVE-002-attempt-2" \
  --id "merge-fix-WAVE-002-attempt-2" \
  --async

Then send the result back to the Reviewer.

4. Maestro’s native review/fix loop

Maestro already models:

execute → review
              ├── PASS/WARN → test
              └── BLOCK → plan --gaps → execute → review

It provides:

quick, standard and deep reviews;
review dimensions such as correctness, architecture and security;
review.json artifacts;
automatic Issue creation for blocking review findings;
plan --gaps for corrective planning;
test --auto-fix loops;
debug for root-cause investigation;
retrospective and learning extraction.

However, the documented review stage is a Maestro Session step, not a stable standalone /review shell command. It is invoked through /maestro, /maestro-next, or a Session chain.

For your custom per-wave lifecycle, maestro delegate --role review is easier to control because you can explicitly select Codex or Claude, choose the integration worktree, and enforce your own JSON contract.

5. How Maestro helps the final Supervisor Agent

After all wave reviews pass, create a final review worktree at the final accepted commit and invoke an independent Codex or Claude session:

maestro delegate \
  "Perform the final goal audit. Compare the complete implementation against context.md and every requirement. Inspect all plans, task files, wave reviews, verification artifacts, and the final code. Write final-audit.json." \
  --to claude \
  --role review \
  --mode analysis \
  --cd "/repo/.worktrees/final-audit" \
  --rule analysis-review-architecture \
  --id "supervisor-final-audit-1" \
  --timeout 1800000 \
  --async

The Supervisor should be a fresh session, not one of the coding or merger sessions. Independence reduces the chance that an agent merely confirms its own previous assumptions.

Recommended final audit output
{
  "audit_id": "FINAL-AUDIT-001",
  "commit_sha": "789abc",
  "verdict": "REPLAN_REQUIRED",
  "requirement_coverage": [
    {
      "requirement_id": "REQ-001",
      "description": "Users can refresh expired access tokens",
      "status": "satisfied",
      "evidence": [
        "src/auth/refresh.ts:20",
        "tests/auth/refresh.test.ts"
      ]
    },
    {
      "requirement_id": "REQ-009",
      "description": "Refresh tokens can be revoked",
      "status": "missing",
      "evidence": [],
      "reason": "No revocation persistence was implemented."
    }
  ],
  "unmet_requirements": ["REQ-009"],
  "planner_feedback": [
    {
      "requirement_ids": ["REQ-009"],
      "problem": "The original plan omitted refresh-token revocation.",
      "recommended_scope": "Create corrective tasks for storage, API behavior and tests."
    }
  ]
}

Do not let the Supervisor return only “satisfactory” or “unsatisfactory.” Require a complete requirement-to-evidence matrix. This makes the final decision reproducible.

6. Returning final gaps to the Planner

When the Supervisor returns REPLAN_REQUIRED, resume the original Planner session if you want it to retain its planning reasoning:

maestro delegate \
  "The final Supervisor found unmet requirements. Read final-audit.json and create a corrective plan revision. Preserve completed tasks, create only the necessary new or superseding tasks, and recalculate dependencies and waves." \
  --to codex \
  --role plan \
  --mode write \
  --cd "/repo" \
  --resume "$PLANNER_SESSION_ID" \
  --id "planner-revision-2" \
  --async

Alternatively, Maestro’s native bridge is:

review/verification gap → plan --gaps → execute → verify

The revised plan should be a delta, not a complete replacement:

{
  "revision": 2,
  "based_on_plan": "PLN-001",
  "triggered_by": "FINAL-AUDIT-001",
  "preserved_completed_tasks": [
    "TASK-001",
    "TASK-002",
    "TASK-003"
  ],
  "new_tasks": [
    "TASK-010",
    "TASK-011"
  ],
  "superseded_tasks": [],
  "unmet_requirements": ["REQ-009"]
}

This prevents the Orchestrator from rerunning already accepted work unnecessarily.

7. Ralph as the overall policy model

Maestro Ralph is particularly relevant to your design. It provides decision gates at:

post-execute;
post-business-test;
post-review;
post-test;
post-goal-audit;
post-milestone.

At each gate, Ralph decides whether to proceed, fix, escalate or stop. It also uses canonical Session/Run/Artifact/Evidence files and supports resumable execution.

Therefore, you have two implementation choices:

Use Ralph as the outer lifecycle policy and extend it with your custom wave/worktree operations.
Implement your own Orchestrator state machine while adopting Ralph’s gate and artifact model.

Your task-level worktree and “resume the exact coder responsible” requirements are more specialized than the supplied Ralph documentation, so the second option will likely be easier initially. Ralph’s behavior is described in the 
command usage guide and 
coordinator guide.

8. State files to add

Extend the earlier orchestration state with reviews and final audits:

.workflow/orchestration/
├── execution-state.json
├── waves/
├── task-results/
├── merge-results/
├── wave-reviews/
│   ├── WAVE-001-attempt-1.json

1:37:| **Chosen Solution** | Agentic RAG with a structured iteration loop (max 6 iterations) and LangChain tool-calling |
2:71:## ADR-006 — LangChain as the LLM Framework Wrapper
3:75:| **Decision** | Use LangChain's `ChatOpenAI` (not native `openai` SDK) as the wrapper for all LLM calls |
4:78:| **Options Considered** | **LangChain `ChatOpenAI`** (provider-agnostic, tool-call handling, broad community) · **Native `openai` SDK** (lower-level, tighter coupling to OpenAI) · **`langchain_groq.ChatGroq`** (Groq-specific, less portable) · **No framework (raw `requests`)** (maximum control, maximum boilerplate) |
5:79:| **Chosen Solution** | `langchain_openai.ChatOpenAI` with `base_url` pointed at Groq's OpenAI-compatible endpoint |
6:80:| **Rationale** | `ChatOpenAI` is provider-agnostic — switching between Groq, OpenAI, local vLLM, or TGI only requires changing `base_url` and `api_key`. The tool-call handling (schema passing, response parsing, multi-turn message threading) is handled by the framework. Using `ChatGroq` was rejected because it would couple the codebase to one provider. LangChain is "glue" — not a model itself — and its overhead is acceptable given the simplification. |
7:81:| **Impact** | All three LLM instances (`llm`, `merge_llm`, `judge_llm`) are built from `ChatOpenAI`. The design proved directly useful when later testing with local TGI servers — only `base_url` and `http_client` needed to change. LangChain's `LLMChainExtractor` was subsequently *rejected* for context compression due to extra latency and dependency on LangChain's retriever format. |
