## 30. LangGraph As A State-Machine Framework For Agentic Pipelines

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | When LangGraph adds value over a hand-rolled state machine for an agentic RAG pipeline, and where it doesn't. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-15 |
| **Findings** | LangGraph is a state-machine framework: nodes are functions, edges are typed transitions (including conditional ones), and a *checkpointer* serialises graph state at node boundaries so a paused or crashed run can resume from the last completed node rather than the start. The framework's marketed benefits — speed, reliability, "agentic-ness" — do not all hold uniformly. **Parallelism**: LangGraph supports parallel branches that merge at a fan-in node, so independent operations (multiple sub-query retrievals; multiple retrieval strategies like BM25 + vector + KG search; per-track validators) can run as `max(branches)` instead of `sum(branches)` — genuine speedup, but only where the work is genuinely independent. **Sequential pipelines**: NAC → DC → LBC → DRAFT → JUDGE is a dependency chain (each step consumes the prior step's output), so LangGraph orchestration cannot parallelise it; runtime is identical to a plain Python `for` loop and the framework adds small but real orchestration overhead. **Checkpointing**: this is the framework's strongest claim. With a `MemorySaver` (RAM-only) checkpointer, LangGraph can recover within a live process — a transient LLM error, a malformed-JSON parse failure, a retryable timeout — without re-running already-completed nodes. With a *durable* checkpointer (SQLite, Postgres, S3, JSON files) it can also recover across process death, deployment, container restart, or human-in-the-loop pauses lasting hours. **Critical limitation**: durability requires the checkpointer to write somewhere persistent *before* the failure occurs; if state lives only in RAM and the process dies, recovery is impossible — LangGraph does not change this fundamental property of computing. **Recovery granularity**: checkpoints are at node boundaries, so a failure inside a node restarts that node from its entry, not from the failing line within it. **Time travel**: a durable checkpoint store lets the user resume from an *earlier* checkpoint, not only the most recent one — useful for "go back and try a different branch" workflows. **Human-in-the-loop**: pause/resume is native; the equivalent in a hand-rolled pipeline requires manual state serialisation and restart plumbing. **Not magic**: LangGraph does not accelerate the LLM calls, embedding calls, or vector DB calls themselves — those still take what they take. The decision criterion is therefore not "does the pipeline have multiple phases" (it does) but "does the pipeline have *independent* phases that would parallelise, *failure modes* the existing retry paths can't already recover from, or *long-running pauses* like human review that survive across deployments." |
| **Conclusion** | For this project today, none of LangGraph's three highest-value features apply: the longest path is sequential, transient failures are already handled by the existing `llm_caller.py` retry/backoff layer (ADR-026), and there is no human-in-the-loop step or long-running pause. The genuine parallel opportunities (sub-queries during RETRIEVE; per-track validation) are smaller than a full framework migration and can be implemented with `asyncio.gather` / a thread pool at much lower cost. Decision: defer LangGraph adoption (ADR-034). Re-open when (a) parallel sub-query retrieval is implemented and shows enough complexity to justify a framework, (b) human-in-the-loop review is added, or (c) workflows must survive process restarts and a durable checkpoint store is on the table anyway. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove ADR-034 (defer LangGraph). Confirms that the `langgraph` declaration already in `requirements.txt` is a transitional artefact rather than an active dependency — a follow-on cleanup can either remove it or wait until a future ADR reverses the decision. Frames the genuine parallel-execution opportunities (parallel sub-query retrieval, parallel per-track validation) as candidates for `asyncio` / thread-pool concurrency rather than framework adoption. Documents the checkpoint-recovery requirement for future workflows that span process lifetimes (e.g. user-disconnect-and-return, deployment-during-workflow). |

---
 
## 31. LangGraph Hands-On Mechanics — `StateGraph` Compilation, Conditional Edges, and Visualization
 
| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | Practical `StateGraph` syntax and tooling gaps discovered while building small graphs by hand, as a follow-up to the theoretical evaluation in Research topic 30. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-16 |
| **Findings** | A `StateGraph` is built from a `TypedDict` state class, `add_node(name, fn)` calls where each node function receives the full state and returns only the fields it updates, and `add_edge`/`add_conditional_edges` calls wiring `START`/`END` sentinels to named nodes. A conditional edge takes a router function that inspects state and returns a string key, which a dict argument maps to the actual next-node name (or `END`) — this is the mechanism behind the no-context early exit wired into `tempFile.py`'s `route_after_retrieve`. The object returned while building the graph (the `StateGraph` itself) and the object returned by `.compile()` are distinct — only the compiled graph exposes `.get_graph()` for inspection/visualization; calling it on the uncompiled builder raises `AttributeError`. Visualization has two paths: `draw_mermaid()` returns plain Mermaid source text with no extra dependencies (viewable at mermaid.live), while `draw_mermaid_png()` renders an actual image but requires `playwright` plus a downloaded Chromium build (`playwright install chromium`) to do the rendering. `IPython.display.Image(...)`/`display(...)` only works inside a Jupyter kernel — in a plain terminal script it is a no-op (or errors), so the correct pattern for a script is to write the PNG bytes returned by `draw_mermaid_png()` to a file with a normal `open(..., "wb")`. Checkpointing for resumability is enabled by compiling with `checkpointer=MemorySaver()` and passing a `thread_id` inside the `config={"configurable": {...}}` argument to `.invoke()` — this is in-memory only and does not survive process death, consistent with the durability caveat already recorded in Research topic 30. A simple state-design heuristic emerged from discussion: "if I checkpoint this graph and resume tomorrow, does this value represent the current progress of this specific run?" — yes means it belongs in graph state (query, retrieved/validated/compressed chunks, draft, iteration/retrieval counters, final answer); no means it's configuration or an injected dependency (the project's 18 `ENABLE_*` flags, `MAX_TOTAL_RETRIEVALS`, `RETRIEVAL_TOP_K`/`_TOP_L`, and service singletons like `EmbeddingManager`, `VectorStore`, `RAGRetriever`, and the LLM clients) and should be passed via `config` or dependency-injected into nodes, not stored in state. |
| **Conclusion** | The mechanics are straightforward once distinguished clearly: state vs. config/dependencies, builder vs. compiled graph, and the two visualization paths (text-only vs. rendered-PNG-via-Playwright). All three mistakes made while building the first toy script (missing `__main__` guard, Jupyter-only display call, `.get_graph()` on the wrong object) were tooling/environment confusions rather than `StateGraph` design errors, suggesting the state/config split is the part actually worth getting right before any production port — consistent with the answer given when asked where the project's 18 config flags should live. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly informed ADR-035 (pause the in-progress migration, practice on disposable graphs first) and the design of the learning sandbox `tempFile.py`, which deliberately wires the project's real `EmbeddingManager`, `VectorStore`, `RAGRetriever`, and `learned_qa_store` into a minimal two-node graph rather than a fully synthetic example, so that the next real migration attempt at `agent_query.py` starts from a working, hands-tested mental model rather than syntax review alone. Builds directly on Research topic 30 (theoretical LangGraph evaluation, 2026-06-15). |

---

## 32. Node vs. Service — The Architectural Boundary in a LangGraph Workflow

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | How to distinguish a LangGraph node from a service, and the correct layering between graph orchestration and reusable business logic. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-18 |
| **Findings** | A **node** belongs to the graph: it receives `state`, reads and writes values in `state`, represents one meaningful stage of the graph, and is connected via edges. It should be thin — its job is orchestration, not computation. A **service** performs reusable work that a node calls; it does not need to know LangGraph exists. The four-layer mental model that emerged from discussion: `Node = workflow orchestration` / `Service = business/technical logic` / `State = changing data between nodes` / `Config = constant architecture settings`. The project's instantiation of this split: nodes live in `app_workflow/nodes/`, services live in `app_workflow/services/` (instances in `services.py`; component logic in individual modules), state in `state.py`, constants in `config.py`. A practical test for the split: *if the code would make sense as a standalone Python function called by any caller — not just LangGraph — it belongs in a service, not a node.* |
| **Conclusion** | Keep nodes thin. Push all compression logic, retrieval logic, validation logic, LLM-calling logic, and prompt-building into service modules. This is consistent with how `app/` already separates `agent_query.py` (orchestration) from `context_compression.py`, `validators.py`, `llm_caller.py`, etc. (business logic). |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove the June 18 restructuring decision (ADR-037): `nodes/nodes.py` was a fat file mixing orchestration and logic; splitting it into per-node files that each call service functions is the correct realisation of this boundary. |

---

## 33. LangGraph State Design — `Annotated` Reducers for Parallel Fan-In and `NotRequired` for Optional Fields

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | How LangGraph resolves conflicting writes from parallel branches, and the two state-field patterns used in `app_workflow/state.py`. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-17 — 2026-06-18 |
| **Findings** | When multiple parallel `Send` branches each return a value for the same state field, LangGraph must decide what to do with multiple concurrent writes. The default behaviour is last-write-wins (the last branch to complete overwrites all prior values). For an accumulation use case — collecting retrieved chunks from multiple parallel sub-query branches — last-write-wins would silently discard all but one branch's results. The solution is `Annotated[list[dict], operator.add]`: wrapping the type with a reducer function tells LangGraph to call `operator.add(existing, new)` on each write, so the field accumulates across all parallel branches rather than being overwritten. This is the mechanism behind `retrieved_document_chunks` and `retrieved_learned_qa_chunks` in `GraphState`. `NotRequired` (from `typing`) is the correct annotation for state fields that are populated by some nodes but not others (e.g. `dedup_merge_document_pairs` is only written by `dedup_merge_documents` and only read by `validate_dedup_merge_documents` — most nodes never touch it). Using `NotRequired` avoids `KeyError` when a node tries to read a field that was never set in the current run. The distinction from Research topic 31's state-vs-config heuristic applies: `Annotated` reducers and `NotRequired` are type-level annotations on *state* fields (values that change during a run); they do not apply to config constants or service singletons. |
| **Conclusion** | Use `Annotated[list[T], operator.add]` for any field that multiple parallel branches write to and whose values should be combined. Use `NotRequired` for any field that only some nodes write. Both are necessary for the dual-track architecture because retrieval runs in parallel fan-out and the per-track validation pairs are only present when validation is enabled. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove the `state.py` design in `app_workflow/`: all `retrieved_*_chunks` fields use `Annotated[..., operator.add]`; all intermediate validation-pair fields (`dedup_merge_document_pairs`, `nac_merge_pairs_documents`, etc.) use `NotRequired`. |

---

## 34. Thread-Based Timeout Pattern in Python — `ThreadPoolExecutor.submit` + `future.result(timeout=N)`

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | How to impose a call-side deadline on a blocking operation that has no native timeout parameter, using only the Python standard library on any platform including Windows. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-22 |
| **Findings** | `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor` provides a cross-platform, library-agnostic timeout mechanism: `executor.submit(fn, *args)` dispatches the call to a background thread and returns a `Future`; `future.result(timeout=N)` blocks the calling thread for at most N seconds. If the future completes within N seconds the result is returned normally; if not, `concurrent.futures.TimeoutError` is raised on the calling thread after exactly N seconds. **Critical caveat**: only the calling thread is unblocked — the background thread that is running `fn` is not cancelled, killed, or interrupted. It continues to completion (or error) in the background, still holding any OS-level resources (sockets, file handles, GPU compute slots) until it finishes. This is unavoidable in CPython's threading model: there is no safe mechanism to kill an arbitrary running thread from outside. Practical consequence for this project: Groq HTTP calls continue until the provider responds (bounded by the provider's own timeout); ChromaDB `.query()` is local and fast; SentenceTransformers' `model.encode()` runs on GPU memory with no network I/O — none of these can deadlock indefinitely, so resource leakage is bounded and acceptable. **Contrast with `signal.alarm(N)`**: POSIX `signal.alarm` can hard-interrupt the process at N seconds, but (a) it is unavailable on Windows and (b) it cannot interrupt calls that hold the GIL, which includes C extensions (NumPy inside SentenceTransformers, ChromaDB's native layer). `ThreadPoolExecutor` sidesteps the GIL constraint entirely because the background thread holds the GIL while executing the call; the calling thread only blocks on the `future.result()` call, which releases the GIL while waiting. **Calibration methodology**: observe wall-clock durations from ten benchmark runs to establish an empirical p95, then apply a safety margin (2× for the LLM where per-call variance is high; ~14× for ChromaDB retrieval and ~500× for embedding where both p95 values are stable sub-second) to set the timeout constant. Margins are deliberately asymmetric — a false positive (triggering a timeout on a legitimately slow-but-correct call) is more disruptive than a generous upper bound. |
| **Conclusion** | Use `ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=1) + future.result(timeout=N)` as the standard pattern for imposing call-side deadlines on blocking I/O that lacks its own timeout parameter. Accept that the background thread continues; verify it cannot deadlock or leak unbounded resources. Calibrate constants from empirical p95 + a multiplier that normal operation will never reach but that pathological hangs will. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove ADR-042 and the June 22 additions to `llm_caller.py`, `retriever.py`, and `embedding_manager.py` in both pipelines. Explains why `signal.alarm` was rejected (Windows + GIL) and why per-library timeout parameters were insufficient (ChromaDB and SentenceTransformers do not expose them). The "background thread continues" caveat informed the decision to avoid this pattern in hot paths that hold exclusive locks or accumulate unbounded resources. Builds on Research topic 34 (this topic) and ADR-042 for the implementation decision record. |

---

## 35. Semaphores vs. FIFO Queue for LLM Call Serialization Under Concurrent Load

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | Whether a `threading.Semaphore` or a `queue.Queue`-based FIFO gate is the correct synchronization primitive for preventing Groq token-quota starvation when multiple threads call `llm_invoke()` concurrently. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-24 |
| **Findings** | **Semaphore approach:** a `threading.Semaphore(1)` limits concurrency to one caller at any moment. The flaw appears when a thread holding the semaphore receives a 429: it must release the semaphore before sleeping the retry delay, otherwise all other threads stall indefinitely. Once it releases, any waiting thread immediately acquires the semaphore and begins burning the token window. When the original 429-holder re-acquires after sleeping, another thread may already have exhausted the quota again. The semaphore provides admission control but not ordering priority: the 429-holder competes equally with new arrivals on every re-acquisition and can cycle through all retries on 429s while new threads continuously starve it. **FIFO queue approach:** each thread places a `threading.Event` on a shared `queue.Queue` and blocks until the current gate-holder sets it. A thread that receives a 429 does NOT release the gate — it holds the event, sleeps the full token-reset window derived from Groq response headers (`x-ratelimit-reset-tokens` → `x-ratelimit-reset-requests` → `retry-after`), then retries directly with no competing calls possible during the sleep. After a successful call, `_gate_release_to_next()` pops the oldest event and wakes the next thread in strict FIFO order. This guarantees: (a) only one thread calls Groq at a time, (b) a 429-holding thread retries with no competing calls during the full reset window, (c) threads are served in arrival order with no starvation. **`asyncio.Lock` misuse:** the prior `async with _query_lock:` in `api.py`'s `/query` handler appeared to serialize at the HTTP layer, but `asyncio.Lock` is not a cross-thread mutex — it is safe only within a single asyncio event loop thread. `asyncio.to_thread()` immediately hands off blocking work to a thread pool, releasing the lock before the pipeline executes. This made the lock entirely ineffective as a concurrency guard for the threading layer where contention occurs. **Adaptive cooldown rationale:** even with a strict FIFO gate, a rapid back-to-back sequence of gate releases (each consuming a different number of tokens) can still briefly breach the per-minute token limit. An adaptive `_cooldown_floor` (derived from `x-ratelimit-remaining-tokens` headers, doubling on 429-recovery, halving on clean-success streak) absorbs this residual without requiring a full 429-cycle wait. |
| **Conclusion** | Semaphores are insufficient for the token-quota exhaustion problem because they provide no guarantee that a 429-holding thread will regain the gate before new arrivals. A FIFO gate where the 429-holding thread retains the gate during its reset-window sleep is the correct primitive: it combines strict ordering, gate retention on failure, and thread-safe semantics (`threading.Lock` + `queue.Queue`). `asyncio.Lock` is not a cross-thread mutex and must not be used for thread pool serialization. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove ADR-044 and ADR-045 and the FIFO gate implementation in both `app/llm_caller.py` and `app_workflow/services/llm_caller.py`. Explains why the prior `threading.Semaphore` design would have re-introduced BUG-053 in a different form, and why `asyncio.Lock` at the `/query` handler level was the root cause of the original failure. |

---

## 36. MongoDB Replica Sets and Multi-Document Transaction Requirements

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | Why MongoDB requires a replica set to support multi-document session transactions, and how to initialise a single-node replica set for local development. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-27 |
| **Findings** | MongoDB's multi-document transactions (`client.start_session()` + `session.start_transaction()`) are only available when the `mongod` instance is running as part of a replica set — they are not supported by a standalone `mongod`. This is a deliberate design constraint: replica sets maintain an oplog (operations log) that is the mechanism MongoDB uses to roll back partial transactions on failure; standalone instances have no oplog. A single-node replica set (often called `rs0`) satisfies the requirement with zero replication overhead and is the standard pattern for local development. To convert a standalone instance: add `replication: replSetName: "rs0"` to `mongod.cfg`, restart the service, then run `rs.initiate()` from a `MongoClient` connected with `directConnection=True`. The `directConnection=True` parameter bypasses PyMongo's topology-discovery phase, which would otherwise fail with `ServerSelectionTimeoutError: Primary()` if the driver tries to discover the primary before `rs.initiate()` has designated one. After `rs.initiate()` completes, standard connections (without `directConnection`) work normally and see a single-node replica set with the local `mongod` as the sole primary. `IndexKeySpecsConflict` errors can arise when two modules attempt to create an index with the same name but different options (e.g. one with `sparse=True` and one without): MongoDB rejects the second call because the existing index spec does not match — see BUG-054. The fix is to ensure all `create_index` calls for the same field produce identical specs across every module that shares the same database. |
| **Conclusion** | For local development, a single-node `rs0` replica set is the correct MongoDB setup when session transactions are needed. The steps are: (1) add `replSetName: "rs0"` to `mongod.cfg`, (2) restart MongoDB, (3) run `rs.initiate()` once via `directConnection=True`. No sharding or additional nodes required. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly required by ADR-048 (MongoDB session transactions for atomic thumbdown write). The `directConnection=True` trick was needed to run `rs.initiate()` successfully after restarting MongoDB with the replica-set config, which caused BUG-054's `IndexKeySpecsConflict` surface during startup. |

## 37. `DuplicateKeyError` as an Idempotency Guard for LangGraph Node Retries

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Topic** | How to use MongoDB's unique-index `DuplicateKeyError` as a low-overhead idempotency guard when a LangGraph node function may be called more than once per graph invocation due to the framework's retry machinery. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-27 |
| **Findings** | LangGraph's `run_with_retry` mechanism (used by graph compilation to handle transient node failures) can re-execute a node function after a non-fatal error. If that node performs a `collection.insert_one(record)` keyed by a field with a unique index (e.g. `request_id`), the second execution will fail with `pymongo.errors.DuplicateKeyError` because the first execution already inserted the document. Without a handler, this exception propagates out of the node and can either crash the graph invocation or trigger another retry loop. The correct handling pattern is `try/except DuplicateKeyError: logger.warning(...); return` — on a duplicate, log a warning (to preserve observability) and return cleanly. The rationale: the first `insert_one` already succeeded and persisted the record; the second call is a no-op from a data-consistency perspective. This pattern converts an otherwise fatal re-insertion error into an explicit, observable idempotency guarantee. The same pattern applies to any insert-once operation in a retry-capable context: it is semantically equivalent to the common HTTP idempotency pattern where a `PUT` with a content-addressed key silently succeeds on a duplicate. For this to work correctly, the key used for the unique index must be stable across retries — `request_id` (a `uuid4` generated once per HTTP request in `api.py` and carried in graph state) satisfies this requirement. |
| **Conclusion** | Wrap any `insert_one` that has a business-level uniqueness constraint (e.g. "one interaction log per request") in `try/except DuplicateKeyError` when the caller is a LangGraph node or any other retry-capable context. Log a warning on duplicate so the retry is visible in logs without surfacing as an error. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove the fix for BUG-055 in both `app/feedback_store.py` and `app_workflow/services/feedback_store.py`. The unique index on `request_id` in `feedback_interactions` was added for correctness (one log per request), but without the `DuplicateKeyError` guard it became a crash path under LangGraph's retry machinery. |
| **Relevance to Project** | Directly drove ADR-044 and the June 24 implementation of `_gate_acquire` / `_gate_release_to_next` / `_apply_cooldown` in `app/llm_caller.py`. Explains why the initial semaphore attempt was discarded and why FIFO ordering is strictly necessary rather than admission-control-only limiting. The FIFO gate was ported to `app_workflow/services/llm_caller.py` on 2026-06-25 (see ADR-045) — both pipelines now use the identical gate, so this research applies equally to both. Builds on Research topic 34 (ThreadPoolExecutor timeout pattern) for the threading model context. |

## ADR-034 · Defer LangGraph Adoption; Keep Hand-Rolled State Machine

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Decision** | Continue with the hand-rolled phase state machine in `agent_query.py` for the foreseeable future. Do not port the pipeline to `LangGraph`. Leave the existing `langgraph` declaration in `requirements.txt` as a transitional artefact until a follow-on cleanup pass either removes it (if no migration is planned in the near term) or imports it (if a future ADR reverses this decision). |
| **Date** | 2026-06-15 |
| **Context** | The pipeline has grown into a multi-phase loop (RETRIEVE → COMPRESS → DRAFT → JUDGE, with iteration budgets, retry paths, thumbdown injection, two-track per-stage handling, and protocol-enforcement nudges). LangGraph is the natural off-the-shelf framework for this kind of workflow: it provides a typed `StateGraph`, named nodes and conditional edges, native checkpointing for resume-after-failure, parallel fan-out branches with automatic merge, human-in-the-loop pause/resume, and time-travel inspection of past checkpoints. The question was whether the current orchestrator's complexity has crossed the threshold where adopting LangGraph would pay off, or whether the hand-rolled state machine is still cheaper to maintain than the framework migration would cost. |
| **Options Considered** | **Port `agent_query.py`'s state machine to a LangGraph `StateGraph` now** (clean architecture, native parallel branches, native checkpointing, but a substantial rewrite of all phase orchestration, protocol enforcement, two-track helpers, and the test harnesses, with no guaranteed runtime speedup). · **Adopt LangGraph only for the parts that benefit most** (parallel sub-query retrieval, parallel per-track validation — but partial adoption means two orchestration patterns coexist in the codebase). · **Build a minimal in-house checkpointing layer** (JSON or SQLite checkpoint per phase boundary — gets the resume-after-failure benefit without LangGraph). · **Defer; keep the hand-rolled state machine** (the chosen option — the present orchestrator is already working, well-traced under the new logging architecture, and the remaining pipeline issues — `BUG-031..037` from the post-separation dry run — are about correctness, not orchestration). |
| **Chosen Solution** | Defer LangGraph adoption. The state machine in `agent_query.py` remains the orchestrator. The `langgraph` package stays in `requirements.txt` as a declared-but-unused dependency until a follow-on cleanup pass either removes it or a future ADR reverses this decision. |
| **Rationale** | LangGraph does not make any individual node faster — its real value is in *parallel fan-out*, *conditional early exit*, *durable checkpoint recovery*, and *human-in-the-loop pause/resume*. For this project's specific phase sequence, the compression/draft/judge tail is strictly sequential (NAC must finish before DC; DC before LBC; LBC before draft; draft before judge) so the framework cannot parallelise the longest path. The genuine parallel opportunities (multiple sub-queries during RETRIEVE; per-track validation during VALIDATE) would benefit from concurrency but can be implemented with `asyncio.gather` or a thread pool without the architectural cost of a framework port. Durable checkpoint recovery requires a persistence backend (SQLite, Postgres, JSON files) that the project doesn't currently use; `InMemorySaver` would only recover within a live process, which the current `try/except` retry paths and the exponential-backoff logic in `llm_caller.py` (ADR-026) already handle for the dominant failure modes (Groq 429, transient HTTP errors). The framework's value rises sharply once the orchestrator includes human-in-the-loop review steps, long-running workflows that may span deployment cycles, or genuinely parallel retrieval strategies — none of which are present today. |
| **Impact** | No code changes today. The decision is documented so that future contributors don't re-litigate the same evaluation. `langgraph` remains a declared dependency until a follow-on cleanup removes it. The decision is explicitly revisitable: when (a) parallel sub-query retrieval is implemented, or (b) human-in-the-loop review enters the pipeline, or (c) long-running workflows that must survive process restarts become a requirement, this ADR should be re-opened. |

## ADR-035 · Pause In-Progress LangGraph Migration; Build Fundamentals via a Throwaway Practice Graph First
 
| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Decision** | Stop the direct, file-by-file migration of `agent_query.py`'s state machine onto LangGraph that had already begun (`app/AgentState.py`), delete the in-progress migration files, and instead build a small, disposable practice graph (`tempFile.py`) wired to the project's real retrieval components to learn `StateGraph` mechanics before resuming any production port. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-16 |
| **Context** | ADR-034 (2026-06-15) deferred LangGraph adoption pending a concrete reason to revisit it, but exploration of the framework's syntax continued the next day and escalated past syntax review into an actual migration attempt: `app/AgentState.py` was created as a full `TypedDict` mirror of the production agent's runtime state (`query`, `messages`, `phase`, both accumulated chunk tracks, token counters, etc.), alongside a toy script (`agent_workflow.py`) used to debug basic `StateGraph` compile/visualize mechanics. This put migration-shaped code into the `app/` package before there was hands-on familiarity with conditional edges, graph compilation, or visualization tooling — the toy script alone surfaced three mechanical mistakes in one sitting (missing `__main__` guard, Jupyter-only `IPython.display` used in a terminal script, calling `.get_graph()` on the uncompiled `StateGraph` instead of the compiled app). |
| **Options Considered** | **Continue the direct migration** (keep building out `AgentState.py` and port `agent_query.py` node-by-node) — fastest path to a finished migration, but ADR-034 was never actually reversed by a concrete driver (no parallel sub-queries, no human-in-the-loop step, no durable-checkpoint need), so building it now would be migrating for the framework's sake rather than a demonstrated benefit. · **Abandon LangGraph entirely, delete everything including the practice work** — consistent with ADR-034's deferral, but discards genuinely useful syntax and mechanics knowledge gained while exploring. · **Pause the production-shaped files; keep practicing on small, disposable graphs wired to real project objects** (the chosen option) — preserves ADR-034's deferral, avoids leaving half-finished migration code in `app/`, and builds the operational familiarity (conditional edges, compilation, visualization, checkpointing) needed to execute a future migration correctly if one is ever undertaken. |
| **Chosen Solution** | Deleted `app/AgentState.py` and `agent_workflow.py`. Created `tempFile.py` at the project root (outside `app/`, importing from it) — a minimal two-node graph (`retrieve` → `generate_answer`) using the project's actual `EmbeddingManager`, `VectorStore`, `RAGRetriever`, and `learned_qa_store` collection, with a conditional edge to `END` when retrieval returns nothing. This file is explicitly non-architectural and will be deleted once it has served its learning purpose. No production module was modified. |
| **Rationale** | ADR-034 already concluded LangGraph offers no benefit to this project's strictly sequential compression/draft/judge tail, and that the genuine opportunities (parallel sub-query retrieval, human-in-the-loop, durable checkpointing) aren't present requirements today. Starting a full migration anyway, before those triggers exist and before a single graph had been run by hand, risked the same class of mistake the toy script immediately exposed (state-vs-config confusion, compiled-vs-uncompiled graph confusion) — but inside the production codebase instead of a disposable file. Practicing against real project objects, rather than a fully synthetic example, gives a closer rehearsal of the eventual migration without the cost of being wrong inside `agent_query.py`. |
| **Impact** | No functional change to the running agent. `app/AgentState.py` and `agent_workflow.py` no longer exist. `tempFile.py` exists at the project root as scratch code, not a tracked architectural module, and should not be treated by future ADRs as a migration artifact — it will be deleted. ADR-034's deferral stands unchanged; this ADR documents the process decision (learn-by-building-small before porting live code) rather than reversing the substantive defer-or-adopt question. Findings from the hands-on session are captured in `Research.md` topic 31. |

## ADR-036 · Adopt LangGraph for the Production Pipeline via a Parallel `app_workflow/` Package

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Decision** | Implement the full production RAG pipeline in LangGraph inside a new `app_workflow/` package, running in parallel with the existing `app/` package. `app/` remains untouched and functional throughout; `app_workflow/` is built incrementally until it reaches feature parity. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-17 |
| **Context** | ADR-034 (2026-06-15) deferred LangGraph adoption because the compression/draft/judge tail is strictly sequential and the genuine parallel opportunities (sub-query retrieval, per-track validation) had not yet been built. ADR-035 (2026-06-16) paused a premature direct migration and substituted a disposable practice sandbox (`tempFile.py`). By 2026-06-17, the sandbox had demonstrated the core LangGraph mechanics (parallel `Send` fan-out, `Annotated` fan-in reducer, conditional edges, `draw_mermaid_png` visualization) against real project objects. The two-track retrieval architecture introduced on 2026-06-11 (ADR-029) also surfaced a concrete parallel opportunity: document and learned-QA tracks can be validated, dedup-merged, and compressed independently and simultaneously — exactly the fan-out/fan-in pattern LangGraph is built for. These two facts together — working hands-on knowledge of LangGraph mechanics, and a confirmed parallel workload — are the triggers ADR-034 identified as the conditions for reversing the deferral. |
| **Options Considered** | **Direct migration — replace `app/agent_query.py` in-place** (fastest path to a single codebase, but risks breaking the working production agent during incremental work). · **Fork `app/` and migrate the fork** (avoids risk to the live agent but creates two diverging copies with no clear boundary). · **New parallel package `app_workflow/` that imports from `app/` for services** (the chosen option — clean separation; `app/` remains a fallback at any point; `app_workflow/` can import `app/`'s service modules rather than duplicating them). · **Abandon LangGraph, implement parallelism with `asyncio.gather` in `agent_query.py`** (lower adoption cost, but foregoes native fan-out/fan-in wiring, conditional early-exit edges, and the graph-topology visualisation the team has found useful for dry-run debugging). |
| **Chosen Solution** | New `app_workflow/` package. Service modules are copied from `app/` into `app_workflow/services/` for now (and `sys.path` patched so `app/` bare imports resolve from `app_workflow/`). Node files live in `app_workflow/nodes/`, one per pipeline stage. Graph wiring in `app_workflow/graph.py`. `app/` is never modified. |
| **Rationale** | The parallel package approach satisfies the two new triggers from ADR-034 without any risk to the working production agent. It also provides a natural gate: `app_workflow/` must reach feature parity with `app/agent_query.py` (including feedback recording, thumbdown persistence, and self-learning) before `app/` can be retired. Running both in parallel makes that parity comparison testable. The LangGraph fan-out/fan-in model is a better architectural fit for the two-track pipeline than hand-rolled helpers: `Annotated[list[dict], operator.add]` on both `retrieved_document_chunks` and `retrieved_learned_qa_chunks` gives parallel accumulation for free, and the two compression track pipelines (documents: NAC→DC→LBC; learned_qa: DC→LBC) converge at `combine_tracks` via native LangGraph fan-in rather than a manual merge call. |
| **Impact** | `app/` continues to run unchanged. `app_workflow/` is the new development target. Two known parity gaps remain open after the initial build (June 17–18): `user_thumbdowns.json` `variants` field is always empty; `failed_variants.json` is never written by `app_workflow/`. Both must be closed before `app/` can be retired. Introduces a `services/` duplication between the two packages that should be consolidated in a follow-on cleanup once parity is confirmed. |

## ADR-037 · Split `app_workflow/nodes/nodes.py` Into Per-Node Files; `config.py` Promoted to Package Root

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Decision** | Replace the monolithic `app_workflow/nodes/nodes.py` (all node functions in one file) with one file per pipeline stage, each importing only its required services. Promote `config.py` from `services/` to the `app_workflow/` root so both `graph.py` and node files can import constants without a circular path through `services/`. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-18 |
| **Context** | The day-1 `nodes/nodes.py` grew to include all REPL command nodes, query variant generation, retrieval, all six compression nodes (NAC/DC/LBC × 2 tracks), dedup-merge, combine-tracks, and answer generation in a single file. It had already accumulated import issues (undefined loggers, stale unused imports, inconsistent relative paths) by the first run. The `config.py` inside `services/` created a circular-import risk: `graph.py` needs routing constants, which live in `config.py`, which is inside the `services/` package that also imports from `graph.py` indirectly via node files. |
| **Options Considered** | **Keep monolithic `nodes.py`** (lower initial overhead but poor for incremental development — adding or modifying one node requires reading the entire file). · **One file per node** (the chosen option — mirrors the `app/` module convention and the standard LangGraph modular layout discussed in the June 17 fundamentals session). · **Group by pipeline phase** (e.g. `retrieval_nodes.py`, `compression_nodes.py`) — a middle ground, but phases don't map cleanly to import dependencies. |
| **Chosen Solution** | One file per conceptual group: `user_input.py`, `commands.py`, `query_variants.py`, `retrieve.py`, `validate_retrieval.py`, `dedup_merge.py`, `nac.py`, `dc.py`, `lbc.py`, `combine_tracks.py`, `generate_answer.py`, `no_context_answer.py`. `nodes/__init__.py` re-exports all public node functions. `nodes/nodes.py` deleted. `config.py` moved from `services/config.py` to `app_workflow/config.py`. |
| **Rationale** | Per-file nodes match the architectural separation the team already uses for `app/` production modules, are consistent with the modular LangGraph layout learnt during the June 16–17 fundamentals sessions, and make it straightforward to find and modify any single pipeline stage without reading hundreds of unrelated lines. Moving `config.py` to the package root resolves the circular-import risk and makes `RETRIEVAL_TOP_K`, `MAX_TOTAL_RETRIEVALS`, and the `ENABLE_*` flags importable from a stable location that both `graph.py` and any node file can reference. |
| **Impact** | `nodes/nodes.py` no longer exists; `nodes/__init__.py` is the import surface for graph wiring. `app_workflow/config.py` is the canonical location for all `app_workflow/`-level configuration constants. Any future node additions follow the one-file-per-node convention. No change to `app/`. |

---
 
## ADR-038 · Split `generate_answer` Into `generate_draft` / `check_answer_quality` / `auto_distillation`, Mirroring `app/agent_query.py`'s Budget-Gated JUDGE Loop
 
| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Decision** | Replace the single `generate_answer` node (which had grown to handle drafting, quality-checking, and answer finalisation in one function) with four nodes — `generate_draft`, `check_answer_quality`, `generate_answer` (now a thin terminal node), and `auto_distillation` — connected by conditional edges that reproduce `app/agent_query.py`'s JUDGE-phase budget check (`budget_ok = ENABLE_SUB_QUERY_GENERATION and retry_count < LLM_RESPONSE_RETRY_LIMIT`) rather than refining the draft inline inside the quality-check node. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-19 |
| **Context** | `generate_answer` was the last unsplit pipeline stage in `app_workflow/`, controlling three independently-flagged behaviours (`ENABLE_ANSWER_DRAFT_CREATION`, `ENABLE_ANSWER_QUALITY_CHECK`, `ENABLE_AUTO_DISTILLATION`) from inside one function body — the same kind of monolith ADR-037 had already split `nodes.py` to avoid at the file level. A first pass wired three new nodes (`draft_answer`, `check_answer_quality`, `auto_distillation`) with simple boolean-flag edges, but `check_answer_quality` refined the draft internally on an `INSUFFICIENT` verdict — which doesn't have a way to re-run retrieval with new query variants, unlike `app/agent_query.py`'s JUDGE phase, which loops back to RETRIEVE when budget remains and only falls back to a best-effort answer when it doesn't. |
| **Options Considered** | **Keep quality-check-with-inline-refinement** (the first pass) — simpler graph, but an `INSUFFICIENT` verdict can only be patched by asking the same LLM to try again with the same context, never by fetching new evidence; this diverges from `app/`'s actual self-correction behaviour. · **Route `INSUFFICIENT` back to `generate_query_variants` when budget remains** (the chosen option) — reproduces the real retry semantics from `app/agent_query.py`, using the graph's existing retry edge and `LLM_RESPONSE_RETRY_LIMIT` rather than inventing a new bound. · **Add a dedicated `refine_draft` node** — closer to a true 1:1 port of the inline refinement, but duplicates logic already covered by re-running the existing `generate_draft` node with fresh context after a retry loop. |
| **Chosen Solution** | `generate_draft.py` only produces `state["draft"]`. `check_answer_quality.py` only runs the judge and stores `state["quality_verdict"]` — no refinement. `routes.py`'s `route_after_quality_check` computes `budget_ok` and returns `"retry"` (→ `generate_query_variants`) when `INSUFFICIENT` with budget remaining, or `"generate_answer"` (best-effort) otherwise. `generate_answer.py` is the single terminal node: uses `state["draft"]` when present, derives `quality` from `quality_verdict`, and always logs feedback. `auto_distillation.py` is a thin one-liner calling `self_learner.run_distillation()`. The originally-named `draft_answer.py` was deleted and replaced by `generate_draft.py` during the redesign. |
| **Rationale** | The graph already has a retry edge (`generate_query_variants` ← retrieval/compression failure paths) and a budget bound (`LLM_RESPONSE_RETRY_LIMIT`) built for exactly this purpose — re-using it for the quality-check loop avoids introducing a second, parallel retry mechanism with separate semantics. Keeping `check_answer_quality` refinement-free also keeps each node single-purpose (judge vs. retry vs. finalise), continuing the per-node-single-responsibility convention from ADR-037 and Research topic 32 (node vs. service boundary). |
| **Impact** | `state.py` gained `draft: NotRequired[str]` and `quality_verdict: NotRequired[str]`. `routes.py` gained `route_after_generate_draft`, `route_after_quality_check`, `route_after_generate_answer`. `graph.py`'s answer pipeline is now `combine_tracks → generate_draft → check_answer_quality → generate_answer → auto_distillation → END`, each arrow conditional on its governing `ENABLE_*` flag. `generate_draft.py` and `generate_answer.py` also converted their `llm.invoke()` calls to `llm_invoke()` for consistency with the rest of `app_workflow/`. |
 
---
 
## ADR-039 · Subprocess-Isolated `benchmark.py` for `app/` vs `app_workflow/` Comparison, Abandoned as Unreliable Under Shared Rate Limits
 
| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Decision** | Build a single `benchmark.py` at the project root that runs a fixed query against either `app_workflow.main` or `app.agent_query` (selected via a `workflow`/`agent` CLI argument) three times each, in isolated subprocesses, appending timing + answer results to a shared `benchmark_results.txt`. After repeated `429` rate-limit storms persisted even with a 2-minute inter-run delay, abandon automated side-by-side benchmarking in favour of manually running each pipeline separately, with pauses, and checking output by hand. |
| **Date** | 2026-06-19 |
| **Context** | With `app_workflow/` having reached rough feature parity with `app/` after the June 17–19 build-out, a direct timing and answer-quality comparison was needed. Both projects define a bare `config` module at their respective roots, so importing both into a single Python process causes module-name collisions — `app.config` and `app_workflow.config` cannot coexist as the same imported name without one shadowing the other when both are added to `sys.path` simultaneously. |
| **Options Considered** | **In-process comparison** (import both pipelines into one script) — simplest code, but blocked by the `config` module collision described above; would require renaming or restructuring one project's modules just for benchmarking, which wasn't acceptable. · **Subprocess isolation per system** (the chosen option) — each worker subprocess only puts its own project's directories on `sys.path`, so no collision occurs; results are passed back via sentinel-delimited stdout (`%%BENCH_ELAPSED%%=`, `%%BENCH_ANS_START%%` / `%%BENCH_ANS_END%%`) parsed by the orchestrator. · **Two separate benchmark scripts, one per system** — avoids the collision without subprocesses, but loses the single-file, single-invocation convenience and makes the side-by-side average comparison harder to keep in sync. |
| **Chosen Solution** | One `benchmark.py` that doubles as orchestrator and worker: `python benchmark.py workflow` or `python benchmark.py agent` runs only the requested system 3 times via `subprocess.run([sys.executable, "benchmark.py", "--_mode=…", QUERY])` and **appends** a self-contained results block to `benchmark_results.txt` (revised from an initial version that ran both systems automatically and overwrote the file). A 2-minute `time.sleep()` was inserted between consecutive runs within one invocation to let Groq's per-minute rate limits recover. |
| **Rationale** | Subprocess isolation is the only option that avoids the `config` module collision without restructuring either production project, and keeps the benchmark tool fully external to both pipelines (no changes needed inside `app/` or `app_workflow/` to support it). Append-mode output lets `python benchmark.py workflow` and `python benchmark.py agent` be run independently (e.g. on different days) while still building up a comparable `benchmark_results.txt`. |
| **Impact** | `benchmark.py` exists as a 321-line standalone tool at the project root; neither `app/` nor `app_workflow/` were modified to support it. Despite the 2-minute inter-run delay, side-by-side runs of both pipelines still triggered Groq `429` rate-limit retry storms (confirmed in `agent_query_20260619_185829_debug.log` and `rag_langgraph_20260619_183828_debug.log`), making wall-clock comparisons unreliable for today's session. No further automated-benchmark work was done after this; manual pipeline-by-pipeline checking with pauses was used instead. The benchmark tool itself is not deprecated — only today's attempt to run it back-to-back without a larger gap between systems. |

## How this project grows

The build order below follows the working pattern from the sibling `RAG-work` project (`RAG-work/docs/Status.md`, April–July 2026), which took a system from nothing to a two-implementation, three-tracing-backend, GPU-microservice architecture without ever having a "big rewrite" phase. Six habits carried that:

1. **Infrastructure before intelligence.** April 2026 built `ingest.py`, `embedding_manager.py`, `vector_store.py`, `retriever.py` — and a plain single-pass `query.py` — *before* the agentic loop existed. The clever part was built on a data layer that already worked.
2. **A walking skeleton that runs end-to-end on real data, early.** `query.py` (non-agentic) preceded `agent_query.py` (4-phase loop). The first dry runs immediately surfaced the two problems that dictated the next month of work (acronym ambiguity, premature tool calls). Failures found by running set the backlog; speculation did not.
3. **Cheap storage first, real storage later.** Interaction history and thumbdowns were JSONL flat files from May until the MongoDB migration on 2026-06-27. The schema was learned by using it, then migrated once.
4. **One focused change per session, logged the same day.** Each dated entry is a single capability plus the bugs it exposed, with ADR and BUG identifiers. Nothing accumulates unrecorded.
5. **Don't adopt a framework until the hand-rolled version shows you what you need.** LangGraph was "researched, not adopted" on 2026-06-15, sandboxed on 06-16/06-17, and only then became a real implementation — built *alongside* the original, sharing one store, and benchmarked head-to-head rather than replacing it.
6. **Observability and optional services come after the core works.** Tracing backends arrived in July; the isolated GPU Marker service arrived on 07-24, once ingestion was stable enough to be worth accelerating.

Applied here, that means: **the durable artifact layer and a one-agent spine come before routing, budgets, or failover** — even though failover is the headline feature. This is also what ADR-005 already concluded independently ("load-bearing … and the first thing to build").

A second framing, from `Architecture.md`, governs every stage: this is *structurally a job scheduler whose workers are unreliable, expensive, and non-deterministic*. When a stage feels like it is about prompting, it has probably been scoped wrong.

---

## Target: the v0 acceptance test

Restated verbatim in substance from `Status.md` (2026-08-03), because it is the clearest definition of done and was not recorded anywhere else:

> Give the system a real task in a real repo, set one agent's budget artificially low so it will be exhausted partway through, and have the system plan, start implementing, detect the limit, fail over to a second agent mid-task, continue from the existing partial work rather than restarting, pass the deterministic check runner, and leave a complete audit trail.

Broken into the assertions a test can actually make:

| #  | Assertion                                                                            | Verified by                                                                                                   |
| -- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| A1 | A plan exists with discrete steps before implementation begins                       | Plan document persisted, steps in`pending` state                                                            |
| A2 | Agent 1 starts work and completes at least one step                                  | ≥1 commit in the worktree attributable to agent 1; ≥1 step`done`                                          |
| A3 | Agent 1's exhaustion is detected, not guessed                                        | Ledger state transitions to`exhausted` from a non-zero exit + rate-limit match, not from the estimate alone |
| A4 | A handoff packet is built **without** agent 1 having written anything on exit | Packet assembled purely from commits, step status, event log, last check result                               |
| A5 | Agent 2 continues rather than restarts                                               | Agent 2's first commit builds on agent 1's HEAD; no step already`done` is re-executed                       |
| A6 | Dead ends are carried forward                                                        | The packet contains agent 1's failed attempts; agent 2 does not repeat the identical failing approach         |
| A7 | Completion is decided by exit codes, not by any agent's claim                        | Check runner result recorded with the commit SHA it ran against                                               |
| A8 | The whole run is reconstructable after the fact                                      | Event log replays the run start-to-finish, including the switch and its reason                                |

**A4 is the test.** A1–A3 and A5–A8 can all pass in a design that still fails the moment a real rate limit kills a process mid-sentence. Kill agent 1 with `SIGKILL` rather than a graceful stop when exercising this.

---

## Stage 0 — Preflight and repository skeleton

**Goal:** the environment is provably capable of what later stages assume, and failures are loud at startup rather than opaque later.

**Why first:** `Bugs.md` #2's Prevention note asks for exactly this — the orchestrator invokes every agent as a subprocess, so each CLI must resolve from *the orchestrator's* environment, not from an interactive shell where PATH was fixed by hand. `Bugs.md` #1 adds the network variant: verify an endpoint from the process that will actually call it.

**Verified present on this machine as of 2026-08-04:**

| Component   | Status                                              |
| ----------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
| Python      | 3.13.13 (`.venv`, per `Status.md` 2026-08-03)   |
| Maestro     | 0.5.61                                              |
| Claude Code | 2.1.220                                             |
| Codex       | codex-cli 0.146.0                                   |
| Git         | 2.52.0.windows.1                                    |
| Docker      | 29.6.2                                              |
| MongoDB     | listening on`localhost:27017` (`mongosh` 2.8.3) |

**Not present:** `gemini`, `opencode`, `aider` are not on PATH. Also note `~/.maestro/cli-tools.json` currently has **only `codex` enabled** (`claude`, `gemini`, `opencode`, `agy` are all `enabled: false`). Delegating to Claude Code requires flipping `claude.enabled` to `true` there. Plan v0 around **Codex + Claude Code**, the two agents that actually exist here; treat Gemini/OpenCode/Aider as Stage 8 adapters.

**Build:**

- `preflight.py` — resolves every configured agent binary, runs each one's version probe, checks MongoDB connectivity, checks `git worktree` support, and exits non-zero with a per-item report. Nothing else runs until this passes.
- `requirements.txt` / `requirements-dev.txt` — these do not exist in the working tree or in git history and must be rewritten. Keep them phase-scoped: Stage 0–3 needs little more than `pymongo`, `pydantic`, and a test runner. Do not pre-install the Phoenix/OTel stack; it belongs to Stage 9.
- Repository layout, created empty and filled by later stages:

```
orchestrator/
├── preflight.py          # Stage 0
├── config.py             # Stage 0 — constants, feature flags, vendor error strings
├── store/                # Stage 1 — MongoDB access, event log, run/step records
├── workspace/            # Stage 1 — git worktree lifecycle + lock
├── checks/               # Stage 2 — deterministic check runner
├── adapters/             # Stage 4 — one module per agent CLI
├── handoff/              # Stage 3 — packet reconstruction
├── ledger/               # Stage 5 — budget accounting
├── router/               # Stage 6 — agent selection
└── skills/<name>/SKILL.md  # ADR-001 — canonical skill source, injected verbatim
```

**Done when:** `python preflight.py` passes on a clean shell, and fails loudly and specifically when an agent binary is renamed away.

---

## Stage 1 — The durable artifact layer

**Goal:** four artifact classes are written *continuously during* a run, such that killing the process at any instant still leaves a recoverable state.

**Why now:** ADR-005 names this the first thing to build. Every later stage reads from it, and it is the only stage whose absence cannot be worked around later — retrofitting durability onto a system that buffers is a rewrite.

**Build the four artifacts from ADR-005 exactly:**

| Artifact          | Requirement                                                                                         | Failure if skipped                                    |
| ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| Worktree commits  | Agent instructed to commit per logical step, **plus** an orchestrator-side auto-commit timer | A killed process leaves no diff                       |
| Plan step status  | `pending` / `in_progress` / `done`, persisted on every transition                             | Cannot tell what was finished from what was attempted |
| Event stream      | Written to MongoDB**as each event arrives**, never buffered to process exit                   | The exact failure mode ADR-005 exists to avoid        |
| Last check result | Tied to the commit SHA it ran against                                                               | A pass/fail with no anchor is not evidence            |

Also build here, because they are storage concerns:

- **Worktree manager** with a **workspace lock — one active agent per worktree.** `Architecture.md` (2026-08-03 changelog) names the absence of this as one of two failure modes that "burn every agent budget at once." It is not a refinement.
- **Crash reconciliation on startup.** ADR-005's second consequence: a run with a start timestamp and no end timestamp is an orphan. Restart must reconcile persisted plan state against *actual Git state*, never assume an interrupted run did nothing.

**Done when:** you can start a long-running write to a worktree, `SIGKILL` the orchestrator, restart it, and have it correctly report which steps completed, what the diff is, and that the run was orphaned — with no summary having been written by anything.

**Test this stage adversarially.** Kill at: mid-commit, between step transition and event write, and during check execution.

---

## Stage 2 — Single-agent end-to-end spine
I have the full picture now. Before writing code, four decisions materially change the design — the notes and diagram leave them open, and I hit a hard blocker on one.

The blocker: Agents Notes.md assigns agents 1, 3, and 4 to the Gemini CLI. Gemini is not installed on this machine and is "enabled": false in ~/.maestro/cli-tools.json.
